Functions of a Factor:
1. Financing of Receivables (1 mark)
o The factor provides immediate cash to the client by purchasing
their book debts or receivables, usually up to 80–90% of the
value.
o This ensures smooth working capital flow.
2. Sales Ledger Administration (1 mark)
o The factor maintains detailed records of the client’s sales,
debtors, and collections, thereby reducing administrative
CO-1,2 ;
b.   burden.   [05]
BL-1   3. Collection of Debts (1 mark)
o The factor undertakes collection of receivables on behalf of the
client, ensuring timely recovery and reducing bad debts.
4. Credit Protection (1 mark)
o In non-recourse factoring, the factor assumes the risk of debtor
default, thereby protecting the client against credit losses.
5. Advisory and Credit Assessment Services (1 mark)
o Factors assess the creditworthiness of buyers and provide
advice to the client regarding credit limits and risk management.
CO-1;   1. Definition of Financial System – (2 Marks)
BL-2   The financial system refers to a set of institutions, instruments, markets, and
services that facilitate the transfer of funds from surplus units (savers) to
deficit units (investors) in the economy. It includes financial intermediaries
(like banks and NBFCs), financial markets (money, capital, forex), financial
instruments (shares, bonds, debentures), and financial services (investment,
insurance, credit rating, etc.).
2. Significance and Role in Economic Development – (3 Marks)
The financial system plays a crucial role in promoting economic growth and
stability through the following functions:
• Mobilization of Savings: Encourages individuals and institutions to
c.   save and channelizes these savings into productive investments.   [05]
•   Efficient Allocation of Resources: Ensures that funds flow to sectors
with the highest returns and productive potential.
•   Facilitating Capital Formation: Converts savings into capital for
business expansion and infrastructure development.
• Promoting Liquidity and Credit Availability: Provides credit and
liquidity to businesses and individuals, enabling smooth functioning of
economic activities.
• Encouraging Entrepreneurship and Innovation: Offers financial
support for new ventures and technological advancements.
• Maintaining Financial Stability: Through regulatory mechanisms, it
prevents systemic risks and supports sustainable growth.
1. Definition (1 Mark):
Fee-based financial services are services for which financial institutions earn
income in the form of fees, commissions, or service charges rather than
interest. These services primarily provide advisory, evaluative, or facilitative
support to clients.
CO-1, 2 ;
d.   2. Identification and Explanation of Chosen Service — Credit Rating (2 [05]
BL-1
Marks):
A credit rating is a service provided by credit rating agencies, where the
creditworthiness of a company, financial instrument, or debt issue is assessed
and assigned a corresponding rating. This rating helps investors make
informed decisions regarding lending or investment.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
3. Functions (1.5 Marks):
• Assessment of Credit Risk: Evaluates the ability of an entity to meet
its financial obligations.
• Standardized Ratings: Uses grades like AAA, AA, A, etc., to indicate
risk levels.
• Fee-Based Service: The agency charges a fee for conducting the
rating.
• Transparency: Provides investors with an independent and reliable
opinion on credit quality.
4. Benefit (0.5 Mark):
A credit rating enhances market confidence, facilitates capital raising for
companies, and helps investors mitigate default risk.
1. Definition of Derivatives (2 Marks)
Derivatives are financial instruments whose value is derived from the price or
value of an underlying asset, such as stocks, bonds, commodities, currencies,
or market indices. They are essentially contracts between parties whose payoff
depends on changes in the underlying asset.
Example: A stock option is a derivative because its price depends on the price
of the underlying stock.
2. Key Characteristics of Derivatives (3 Marks)
Derivatives have the following essential features:
1. Underlying Asset: The value depends on an underlying asset (equity,
commodity, interest rate, or index).
2. Leverage: Small investment in derivatives can control a large
position in the underlying, amplifying gains and losses.
3. Contractual Nature: They are legally binding agreements between
two parties.
4. Standardization/Customization: Can be exchange-traded
(standardized) or over-the-counter (customized).
5. Hedging and Speculation: Used to hedge risk or speculate for profit.
6. Settlement: Can be cash-settled or physically settled.

Illustration:
• Futures: An investor agrees to buy 100 shares of XYZ at ₹1,000 in 1
month. If the price rises to ₹1,100, they gain ₹10,000; if it falls to
₹900, they lose ₹10,000.
• Options: An investor buys a call option for ₹50 premium to buy XYZ
at ₹1,000. If the stock rises to ₹1,100, profit = ₹1,100 − ₹1,000 − ₹50
= ₹50. If the stock falls, loss is limited to ₹50 (premium).
1. Concept of Leasing (3 marks)
•   Definition: Leasing is a contract in which the owner of an asset (lessor)
allows another party (lessee) to use the asset for a specified period in
return for periodic payments called lease rentals.
• Ownership of the asset generally remains with the lessor during the
lease term.
• Example: A company leases machinery from a financial institution
instead of purchasing it outright.
Major Types of Leasing:
1. Operating Lease: Short-term, the lessor maintains the asset, used for
flexible financing.
2. Finance/Capital Lease: Long-term, lessee bears maintenance and
risks, similar to purchase.
Advantages:
• No upfront capital required.
• Access to modern technology without ownership burden.
• Lease rentals are tax-deductible.
Limitations:
•   Total cost over time may exceed the purchase.
• No ownership benefits unless lease has a purchase option.
CO-1,2;   2. Concept of Hire Purchase (3 marks)
b   [10]
BL-4   •   Definition: Hire purchase is a method of buying an asset by paying in
instalments. Ownership is transferred to the buyer only after full
payment of the price.
• The buyer can use the asset while paying, but legal ownership remains
with the seller until the last instalment.
• Example: A company buys a truck by paying 25% upfront and the rest
in monthly instalments.
Major Types of Hire Purchase:
1. Normal Hire Purchase: Fixed instalments until ownership is
transferred.
2. Conditional Hire Purchase: Transfer of ownership depends on
fulfilling certain conditions (e.g., timely payments).
Advantages:
• Allows asset acquisition without full upfront payment.
•   Easier budget management due to instalments.
• Ownership transfers at the end of term.
Limitations:
• Higher total cost due to interest.
• Asset is legally owned by the seller until last instalment.
•   Default can lead to repossession.
3. Differences Between Leasing and Hire Purchase (4 marks)

(a) Money Market
Meaning (3 marks):
The money market is a segment of the financial system where short-term funds
are borrowed and lent, typically for a period of one year or less. It deals with
highly liquid and low-risk instruments such as Treasury bills, commercial
papers, certificates of deposit, and call money.
Role in Economic Growth (2 marks):
• Provides liquidity to banks, businesses, and the government for short-
term needs.
•   Facilitates efficient allocation of short-term funds, ensuring smooth
functioning of trade and commerce.
• Helps the Central Bank implement monetary policy, controlling
inflation and stabilizing the economy.
Example: The RBI’s call money market allows banks to borrow overnight
funds to meet short-term liquidity requirements.
Q3
CO-1, 2;   a   [10]
(b) Capital Market
BL-1   Meaning (3 marks):
The capital market is a financial market where long-term funds (more than one
year) are mobilized for investment in equity and debt instruments. It comprises
the primary market (issuance of new securities) and secondary market (trading
of existing securities).
Role in Economic Growth (2 marks):
• Mobilizes long-term savings of individuals and institutions for
productive investments.
• Provides funding for infrastructure, industry, and government projects,
fostering growth.
• Enhances capital formation and encourages entrepreneurship.
• Promotes market-based price discovery, improving transparency and
efficiency.
Example: The National Stock Exchange (NSE) and Bombay Stock Exchange
(BSE) facilitate secondary trading of shares and bonds.
1. Mortgage
Definition (2 Marks):
A mortgage is a financial arrangement in which a borrower pledges immovable
CO-1,2 ;   property (like land or building) as security for a loan. The lender has the right
b   [10]
BL-2   to take possession of the property if the borrower fails to repay the debt
according to agreed terms.
•   In India, mortgages are governed under the Transfer of Property Act,
1882.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
• The ownership of the property remains with the borrower, but the
lender holds a legal charge over it until repayment.
Types of Mortgages (2 Mark):
1. Simple Mortgage: Borrower transfers property to lender, but
possession remains with borrower.
2. English Mortgage: Borrower transfers property ownership to the
lender, with condition to re-transfer after repayment.
3. Mortgage by Deposit of Title Deeds (Equitable Mortgage):
Borrower deposits property documents with the lender as security.
Example (1 Marks):
• Mr. Sharma takes a loan of ₹50 lakh from a bank to buy a house. The
house itself is mortgaged to the bank. If Mr. Sharma defaults, the bank
can sell the house to recover the loan.
2. De-listing of Securities
Definition (2 Marks):
De-listing refers to the removal of a company’s securities (shares or
debentures) from a stock exchange, either voluntarily or involuntarily. After
de-listing, these securities are no longer traded on the exchange, although they
may still be traded over-the-counter (OTC).
Types (1 Mark):
1. Voluntary De-listing: Initiated by the company, usually to reduce
compliance costs or go private.
2. Compulsory De-listing: Imposed by the stock exchange if the
company fails to meet listing requirements.
Process (1 Mark):
• Voluntary delisting requires approval of shareholders via a special
resolution.
• The company must buy back shares from public shareholders at a fair
price as per SEBI guidelines.
Example (1 Mark):
•   In 2018, Ranbaxy Laboratories was voluntarily delisted from the BSE
after being acquired by Sun Pharma, which bought out public
shareholders at a fair price.
1. Introduction (1 Mark)
Non-Governmental Organisations (NGOs) play a pivotal role in promoting
microfinance in India by facilitating financial services for the underserved and
financially excluded sections of society, primarily in rural and semi-urban
areas.
2. Role of NGOs in Microfinance (4 Marks)
1. Financial Inclusion:
NGOs bridge the gap between formal financial institutions and the poor
Q4   by providing small loans, savings facilities, and insurance, enabling
CO-1,2;   a   access to credit for those excluded from the formal banking system.   [10]
BL-2   2. Credit without Collateral:
NGOs typically provide microloans without requiring traditional
collateral, relying on social collateral such as community groups to
ensure repayment.
3. Capacity Building:
NGOs often offer training in financial literacy, entrepreneurship, and
income-generating activities, empowering borrowers to utilize loans
effectively.
4. Group Lending Mechanisms:
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
NGOs organize borrowers into Self-Help Groups (SHGs) or joint
liability groups, fostering mutual accountability and higher repayment
rates.
5. Monitoring and Support:
NGOs provide continuous monitoring and guidance, ensuring
sustainable utilization of funds and reducing defaults.
3. Major Model of Microfinance in India – Self-Help Group (SHG) Model
(4 Marks)
a. Description:
• The SHG-Bank Linkage Model is the most prominent microfinance
model in India.
• Groups of 10–20 individuals (mostly women) come together to form a
SHG.
• The SHG collects savings from its members and accesses bank credit
as a group, which is then disbursed to members as loans.
• Repayment and interest are managed collectively, ensuring discipline
and trust.
b. Example: (1 Mark)
• SEWA   (Self-Employed Women’s   Association),   Gujarat:
SEWA promotes SHGs among women workers, providing them access
to microcredit, savings, and insurance. This model has empowered
women economically and socially by enabling entrepreneurship and
financial independence.
1. Introduction (1 mark)
Corporate finance refers to the ways in which a firm raises funds to
finance its operations, expansion, and other activities. The main
sources are Debt and Equity. Both have distinct features, advantages,
and risks.
2. Debt Financing (3 marks)
Definition:
Debt is borrowed capital that a firm must repay at a specified future date,
usually with interest.
Key Features:
• Obligation to repay: Principal + interest is fixed.
• No ownership dilution: Lenders do not get ownership rights.
•   Priority in claims: Debt holders are paid before equity shareholders
CO-1, 2;
b   in liquidation.   [10]
BL-2   Examples:
• Bank loans
• Corporate bonds (e.g., Reliance Industries issuing bonds)
• Debentures
Implications:
• For issuer: Fixed financial burden; can improve Return on Equity
(ROE) if used efficiently; excessive debt increases financial risk.
• For investor: Lower risk than equity; receives fixed interest; limited
upside; priority in case of liquidation.
Risks:
•   Default risk for the issuer
•   Interest rate risk for investors
3. Equity Financing (3 marks)
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
Definition:
Equity is capital raised by issuing shares of the company, representing
ownership interest.
Key Features:
• Ownership rights: Shareholders have voting rights and claim on
residual profits.
• Dividend uncertainty: No fixed return; depends on profits.
• Residual claim: Paid after debt holders in case of liquidation.
Examples:
• Common shares (e.g., Infosys issuing new shares for expansion)
•   Preference shares
Implications:
• For issuer: No fixed repayment; helps reduce financial risk; may dilute
control.
• For investor: Higher potential returns via dividends and capital gains;
higher risk; may influence company decisions through voting rights.
Risks:
• Market risk: Share price fluctuations
• Dividend uncertainty
4. Comparison Table (3 marks)
Feature   Debt   Equity
Ownership   No   Yes
Obligation to repay Yes, fixed   No, residual
Risk to investor   Lower   Higher
Return to investor   Fixed interest   Dividends + capital gains
Claim in liquidation Priority   Residual
Example   Bonds, bank loans Shares, preference shares
1. Introduction (2 marks)
The financial system of India comprises banking and non-banking
financial institutions (NBFIs), both playing a vital role in mobilizing
resources and ensuring economic stability.
• While banks focus on deposit mobilization and credit creation,
• NBFIs complement them by channelizing savings into long-term
investments and serving sectors inadequately covered by banks.
2. Role of Banking Institutions (3 marks)
Banks are the core of the financial system, regulated by the Reserve
Q5   Bank of India (RBI). Their role in resource mobilization includes:
CO-1,2;   a   1. Deposit Mobilization: Collect savings from households and [10]
BL-2,4   businesses through demand and time deposits.
2. Credit Allocation: Provide short- and medium-term loans to
agriculture, industry, and trade.
3. Monetary Stability: Support RBI in controlling money supply and
maintaining price stability.
4. Financial Inclusion: Promote inclusive growth through initiatives like
Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana and Priority Sector Lending.
Example:
• State Bank of India (SBI) mobilizes savings from rural and urban
areas and channels them into productive sectors such as MSMEs and
agriculture.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
3. Role of Non-Banking Financial Institutions (3 marks)
NBFIs, regulated by the RBI and other bodies like SEBI and IRDAI,
supplement the banking sector by mobilizing and deploying funds for
long-term development.
1. Capital Formation: Mobilize household and corporate savings into
investments in infrastructure and industry.
2. Sectoral Support: Provide funds to sectors where banks have limited
exposure, e.g., housing, infrastructure, and small-scale industries.
3. Financial Market Development: Encourage growth of money,
capital, and insurance markets.
Example:
• LIC of India mobilizes long-term savings through life insurance
premiums and invests in infrastructure and government securities.
• NABARD channels rural savings into agricultural development
projects.

1. Meaning of Forfaiting (3 marks)
Forfaiting is a method of trade finance where an exporter sells its medium-
to long-term receivables (debt instruments like bills of exchange,
promissory notes, or letters of credit) to a forfeiter at a discount on a
“without recourse” basis.
•   It is widely used in international trade, particularly in capital goods
exports or projects involving deferred payment.
• The exporter receives immediate cash, while the forfeiter assumes the
risk of non-payment by the importer.
Features:
CO-1, 2;
b   •   Credit risk transferred to the forfeiter.   [10]
BL-2
• Payment is usually in foreign currency.
• “Without recourse” implies the exporter cannot be asked to pay if the
importer defaults.
2. Functions of a Forfeiter (3 marks)
The forfeiter is typically a financial institution (like a bank or specialized
forfaiting company).
Its main functions are:
1. Purchasing Receivables: Buys the exporter’s receivables at a
discount.
2. Assuming Credit Risk: Bears the risk of non-payment by the importer.
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
3. Financing the Exporter: Provides immediate liquidity to the exporter.
4. Collection: Sometimes collects payment from the importer when due.
5. Currency and Interest Risk Management: May offer foreign
exchange and interest rate management services.
3. Mechanism of Forfaiting (4 marks)
The typical steps involved in forfaiting are:
1. Export Contract: Exporter and importer agree on deferred payment
terms.
2. Negotiation with Forfeiter: Exporter approaches a forfeiter to sell the
receivables.
3. Discounting: Forfeiter buys the receivables at a discount (depending
on maturity, credit rating of the importer, and interest rate).
4. Transfer of Receivables: Legal ownership of the debt instrument is
transferred to the forfeiter without recourse.
5. Payment to Exporter: Exporter receives immediate cash, usually net
of discount and fees.
6. Collection from Importer: Forfeiter collects payment from the
importer on the due dates.
Flow: Exporter → Forfeiter → Importer
Advantages:
• Immediate cash flow for the exporter
• Risk of default borne by the forfeiter
•   Facilitates international trade with credit terms
1. Structure of the Indian Financial System (5 Marks)
Definition:
The financial system is the network of institutions, markets, instruments, and
services that facilitate the transfer of funds from savers to borrowers.
Structure:
The Indian financial system can be classified broadly into two segments:
A. Financial Institutions (Organized channels for funds flow)
1. Banking Institutions:
o Commercial banks (public, private, foreign)
o Cooperative banks
o Regional Rural Banks
Q6   2. Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs):
o Provide credit, leasing, hire-purchase, etc.
CO-1   a   [10]
3. Development Financial Institutions (DFIs):
BL-2   o   E.g., SIDBI, NABARD, Exim Bank – support industrial,
agricultural, and export finance.
B. Financial Markets:
1. Money Market: Short-term funds (treasury bills, commercial paper)
2. Capital Market: Long-term funds (equity, debentures, bonds)
o Primary market (new securities)
o Secondary market (trading of existing securities)
C. Financial Instruments:
•   Equity, bonds, derivatives, mutual funds, insurance products
D. Regulatory Bodies:
• RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA – ensure stability, transparency, and
investor protection.

Fund-based financial services are those services where financial institutions
provide funds directly to clients and earn income in the form of interest,
dividends, or capital gains. Two key examples in India are:
Venture Capital Finance (5 Marks)
• Explanation: Venture capital finance involves providing long-term
equity or quasi-equity funds to start-ups and small businesses with high
growth potential, usually in exchange for ownership stakes.
• Contribution to growth:
o Promotes entrepreneurship and innovation, particularly in
technology and emerging sectors.
o Mobilizes private savings into productive investments,
CO-1, 2;   increasing financial activity.
b   [10]
BL-1   o Helps start-ups scale operations, generating more financial
transactions and economic growth.
Mutual Fund Financing (5 Marks)
• Explanation: Mutual funds pool savings from multiple investors and
invest in stocks, bonds, or other securities, providing investors with
professional fund management.
• Contribution to growth:
o Expands access to financial markets for small investors,
increasing participation.
o Encourages systematic savings and capital formation.
o   Facilitates large-scale investment flows into corporate and
government securities, boosting overall financial transactions.
Introduction
• The Indian financial system comprises institutions, instruments, and
regulations that mobilize savings and allocate resources efficiently.
• AMFI and PFRDA are key regulatory and developmental bodies in the
Q7   financial system: AMFI for mutual funds and PFRDA for pension
schemes.
CO-1,2;   a   [10]
1. Association of Mutual Funds of India (AMFI)
BL-1, 2   Role: (1.0 marks)
• AMFI is a self-regulatory organization (SRO) for mutual funds in
India.
•   Registered with SEBI, it promotes ethical practices and investor
education in the mutual fund industry.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Impact:
1. Investor Protection (1.5 marks)
o Standardizes code of conduct for mutual fund distributors.
o Conducts awareness programs to improve financial literacy.
o Maintains grievance redressal mechanisms for investors.
2. Market Development (1 mark)
o Encourages growth of mutual fund penetration.
o   Facilitates innovation in products like hybrid, thematic, and SIP
schemes.
3. Regulatory Compliance (1.5 marks)
o Monitors adherence to SEBI regulations.
o Collects data on industry performance to assist SEBI in policy
formulation.
o Maintains a transparent reporting system for mutual fund
houses.
2. Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA)
Role: (1.0 marks)
• PFRDA regulates the pension sector in India, including the National
Pension System (NPS).
• Focuses on promotion, regulation, and supervision of pension funds to
ensure financial security post-retirement.
Impact:
1. Investor Protection (1.5 marks)
o Safeguards   subscribers’   interests   through   standardized
investment options and disclosure norms.
o Ensures transparency and accountability of Pension Fund
Managers (PFMs).
2. Market Development (1 mark)
o Expands retirement savings coverage, especially among
unorganized sector employees.
o Promotes long-term capital formation, supporting equity and
debt markets.
3. Regulatory Compliance (1.5 marks)
o Frames regulations for PFMs, intermediaries, and custodians.
o Monitors investment guidelines, risk management, and
compliance reporting.
o   Facilitates audit and grievance mechanisms to strengthen
confidence in the pension sector.
1. Meaning/Concept of Leasing (3 marks)
•   Definition: Leasing is a contractual arrangement in which the owner
of an asset (lessor) allows another party (lessee) to use the asset for a
specific period in return for periodic payments called lease rentals.
• The lessee does not acquire ownership immediately, but enjoys the
benefits of using the asset. Ownership may or may not transfer at the
CO-1, 2;
b   end of the lease, depending on the type (operating lease or finance [10]
BL-1,2
lease).
• Leasing is a form of asset financing that helps firm’s conserve capital
while accessing modern equipment.
2. Advantages of Leasing (3 marks)
1. Preserves capital and liquidity: Firms can use expensive assets
without large upfront investment.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
2. Flexibility in upgrading assets: Leases often allow easy replacement
or upgrade to newer technology.
3. Tax benefits: Lease payments are often deductible as business
expenses, reducing taxable income.
4. Reduces obsolescence risk: Especially relevant for technology-
intensive assets.
5. Improves cash flow management: Periodic payments spread over
time instead of lump-sum purchase.
3. Disadvantages of Leasing (2 marks)
1. Higher long-term cost: Total lease payments may exceed the cost of
outright purchase over time.
2. No ownership until end: Lessee does not have asset ownership
initially, limiting collateral options.
3. Obligation to pay: Lessee must continue payments even if asset is
underutilized or business conditions change.
4. Limited customization: Leased assets may have restrictions on
modification or usage.
4. Role of Leasing in Efficient Asset Acquisition (2 marks)
• Leasing allows efficient allocation of resources by letting firms access
essential assets without straining capital.
• Supports capital budgeting flexibility as firms can conserve funds for
other investments.
• Helps businesses adopt latest technology quickly, reducing downtime
and obsolescence.
• Enhances financial planning, as lease payments are predictable and
structured.
********** 13 **********
````

Q1   [20]
Definition of Financial Inclusion (1 mark)   [05]
a.
Financial inclusion refers to the process of ensuring access to
appropriate, affordable, and timely financial services such as banking,
credit, insurance, and payment services to all sections of society,
particularly the vulnerable and low-income groups, at a reasonable cost
in a transparent manner.
Initiatives to Enlarge Financial Inclusion in India
(4 Marks; Any Four; 1 mark each)
1. Banking Outreach & No-Frills Accounts
o Simplified accounts with zero or minimum balance
requirements to bring unbanked people into the formal
CO-1,2;   system.
BL-2   2. Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY)
o Massive financial inclusion programme (2014) focusing
on universal banking access, RuPay cards, overdraft
facilities, and direct benefit transfers (DBT).
3. Business Correspondent (BC) Model
o Use of agents to provide banking services in remote and
rural areas, reducing the need for physical bank
branches.
4. Digital Payment Initiatives
o Introduction of UPI, AEPS, mobile banking, and RuPay
cards to promote cashless and accessible financial
transactions.
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
5. Direct Benefit Transfer
o Transfer   of government   subsidies   directly   into
beneficiaries’ bank accounts, improving transparency
and inclusion.
6. Microfinance & Self-Help Groups (SHGs)
o Provision of small loans to low-income individuals,
especially women,   through SHG-Bank   linkage
programmes.
7. Financial Literacy and Credit Counselling
o Efforts by RBI and other institutions to educate people
about financial products and responsible usage.
A firm should prefer recourse factoring over non-recourse factoring   [05]
in situations where cost minimisation and stronger control over credit
risk are important. Under recourse factoring, the firm retains the risk
of bad debts, making it cheaper than non-recourse factoring.
Situations: (Any two; 2.5 marks each)
1. When the firm has strong credit control systems
If the firm is confident about its customers’ creditworthiness
and has efficient receivables management, it can bear the credit
risk and opt for the cheaper recourse option.
2. When   the   incidence   of   bad   debts   is   low
Firms dealing with reliable, long-term customers or operating
CO-1,2 ;   in stable industries face minimal default risk, making recourse
b.
BL-1   factoring more suitable.
3. When   cost   considerations   are   critical
Recourse factoring involves lower factoring charges since the
factor does not assume credit risk, helping firms reduce
financing costs.
4. When the firm wants only financing and collection services
If the firm does not require credit risk protection but only
liquidity and receivables management, recourse factoring is
appropriate.
5. When the firm is willing to retain risk for higher returns
By bearing the risk, firms avoid higher fees and can improve
profitability compared to non-recourse arrangements.
CO-1; BL-   Major Challenges of the Indian Financial System   [05]
1   c.
(5 Marks; Any Five; 1 mark each)
1. High   Level   of   Non-Performing   Assets   (NPAs)
Indian banks, particularly public sector banks, have historically
faced high NPAs, which have weakened profitability, reduced
lending capacity, and affected financial stability.
2. Financial   Exclusion
Despite initiatives like Jan Dhan Yojana, a significant portion
of the population still lacks access to formal financial services,
especially in rural and informal sectors.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
3. Inefficient   Financial   Intermediation
The transmission of monetary policy is often weak due to rigid
interest rates, structural bottlenecks, and dominance of banks
over capital markets.
4. Underdeveloped   Capital   Markets
Compared to advanced economies, India’s corporate bond
market remains shallow, limiting long-term financing options
for infrastructure and industry.
5. Regulatory   and   Structural   Issues
Multiplicity of regulators (RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, etc.) and
coordination challenges can lead to regulatory overlaps and
gaps.
Mortgage (2 marks)   [05]
d.
A mortgage is the transfer of an interest in specific immovable property
(such as land or building) by a borrower (mortgagor) to a lender
(mortgagee) as security for a loan. The ownership is not fully
transferred; only a limited interest is created to secure repayment.
Upon repayment of the loan, the interest is extinguished, and the
property reverts fully to the borrower.
One Mortgage Instrument (3 marks):
A simple mortgage is one of the most common forms of mortgage in
India.
CO-1, 2 ;   Features:
BL-2   1. The mortgagor does not deliver possession of the property to
the lender.
2. The borrower personally binds himself to repay the loan.
3. In case of default, the mortgagee has the right to cause the
property to be sold through legal process.
4. The lender cannot take possession but can approach the court
for recovery.
Example:
A person takes a housing loan from a bank and creates a charge on their
house as security. If he fails to repay, the bank can sell the property
through legal means.
a   Debt Instruments (5 marks)
Meaning:
Debt instruments represent a contractual obligation whereby the issuer
(borrower) promises to pay periodic interest and repay the principal to
the investor (lender).
Q2   Types in India:
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-2   • Government Securities (G-Secs): Issued by Central/State
Governments
• Corporate Bonds/Debentures: Issued by companies
• Money Market Instruments: Treasury Bills, Commercial
Paper (CP), Certificates of Deposit (CD)
Features:
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
• Fixed income (interest-bearing)
• Defined maturity
• Lower risk (especially G-Secs)
Role in the Indian Financial System:
• Mobilization of savings: Channels household and institutional
savings into productive uses
• Financing fiscal deficit: Government relies on G-Secs
• Benchmark for interest rates: G-Sec yields act as a reference
for pricing other instruments
• Liquidity management: Used by RBI in Open Market
Operations (OMO)
Derivatives (5 marks)
Meaning:
Derivatives are financial contracts whose value is derived from an
underlying asset such as equity, interest rates, currency, or
commodities.
Types in India:
•   Futures and Options (Equity & Index derivatives)
• Currency derivatives
•   Interest Rate Futures (IRFs)
Features:
• No initial full investment (margin-based trading)
• Used for hedging, speculation, and arbitrage
• High leverage
Role in the Indian Financial System:
• Risk management: Helps firms hedge against price, interest
rate, and exchange rate risks
• Price discovery: Reflects market expectations about future
prices
• Market efficiency: Enhances liquidity and depth of financial
markets
• Integration with global markets: Aligns Indian markets with
international practices
b   1. Venture Capital (1 Mark)
Venture Capital (VC) refers to risk capital provided to high-growth,
innovative Startups and small businesses with long-term potential. It is
typically equity-based financing, where investors participate in both
risk and returns.
2. Role of Venture Capital in the Indian Financial System
CO-1,2;
(3 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each) [10]
BL-3
Venture capital plays a pivotal role in strengthening India’s financial
architecture:
• Bridging Financing Gap: Provides funds to startups lacking
access to traditional sources like banks or capital markets.
• Promoting Innovation: Encourages technology-driven and
knowledge-based industries (e.g., fintech, biotech).
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
• Employment Generation: Supports startups that create jobs
and boost economic development.
• Capital Market Development: Successful VC-backed firms
often go for IPOs, deepening capital markets.
• Risk Diversification: Channels funds into high-risk, high-
return ventures, improving allocation efficiency.
3. Importance for Entrepreneurial Growth
(3 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each)
Venture capital is crucial for entrepreneurship due to the following
reasons:
• Access to Risk Capital: Entrepreneurs often lack collateral;
VC provides funding without requiring security.
• Managerial & Technical Support: VC firms offer mentorship,
governance, and strategic guidance.
• Market Credibility: Association with reputed VC firms
enhances trust among stakeholders.
• Long-Term Orientation: Unlike banks, VC investors are
patient and focus on growth rather than immediate returns.
• Encouragement of Innovation Ecosystem: Supports Startups
in emerging sectors, fostering a culture of innovation in India.
4. Stages of Venture Capital Financing (3 Marks)
Venture capital financing typically proceeds through the following
stages:
(i) Seed Stage
•   Initial funding for idea development, research, and prototype
creation.
• High risk, as the business model is not yet proven.
(ii) Start-up Stage
• Financing for product development and initial marketing.
• The firm begins operations but may not generate profits.
(iii) Early Growth Stage (First Stage Financing)
• Funds used for scaling production and expanding market reach.
• Company starts generating revenue.
(iv) Expansion Stage (Second/Third Stage Financing)
•   Capital for expansion, diversification, and increasing capacity.
• Risk reduces as the business stabilizes.
(v) Exit Stage
• VC investors exit through IPO, mergers, or acquisitions.
•   Realization of returns on investment.
An Initial Public Offer (IPO) is the first sale of securities by a company
a   to the public in the primary market. The investment banker (also called
merchant banker) plays a central role in managing, structuring, and
Q3
CO-1, 2;   executing the issue.   [10]
BL-3   1. Role of Investment Banker in IPO
(4 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each)
As an investment banker, I would assist the company in the following
ways:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
(a) Issue Planning & Structuring
• Assess capital requirements and decide issue size, pricing, and
timing
• Choose between fixed price issue or book-building method
(b) Due Diligence
• Conduct financial, legal, and business verification
• Ensure compliance with Securities and Exchange Board of
India regulations
(c) Drafting Offer Document
• Prepare Draft Red Herring Prospectus (DRHP)
•   Disclose all material information for investor protection
(d) Underwriting & Syndication
• Arrange underwriters to guarantee subscription
• Appoint intermediaries: brokers, registrars, bankers
(e) Marketing the Issue
• Conduct roadshows, investor meetings, and advertisements
• Build investor confidence and demand
2. Steps in Public Issue Process (6 Marks)
The IPO process involves the following sequential steps:
(1) Appointment of Intermediaries
• Merchant banker, registrar, underwriters, legal advisors are
appointed
(2) Preparation & Filing of DRHP
•   Draft prospectus submitted to SEBI for approval
• SEBI reviews disclosures and compliance
(3) SEBI Approval
• Observations given by SEBI
• Company incorporates required changes
(4) Pricing of Issue
•   Final price determined:
o Fixed price OR
o Book building process (price discovery through bids)
(5) Marketing & Roadshows
• Promotion through institutional and retail investor outreach
(6) Opening of IPO
•   Public subscription opens for a fixed period (usually 3–5 days)
(7) Allotment of Shares
•   Basis of allotment finalized (oversubscription handled
proportionately)
(8) Listing on Stock Exchange
• Shares listed on exchanges like the National Stock Exchange or
Bombay Stock Exchange
• Trading begins in the secondary market
CO-1,2 ;   b   Buyback of Shares (5 Marks)
[10]
BL-2   Meaning (1 Mark)
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Buyback of shares refers to the process by which a company
repurchases   its own shares from existing shareholders at a
predetermined price, as per regulations laid down by the Securities and
Exchange Board of India.
Features (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Can be carried out through tender offer, open market purchase,
or book-building process
• Governed by SEBI (Buyback of Securities) Regulations
• Leads to a reduction in share capital
• Improves earnings per share (EPS) and return ratios
•   Subject to limits on debt-equity ratio and quantum of buyback
Significance (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
•   Signals confidence of management in company prospects
• Helps in price stabilization during market volatility
• Provides an exit opportunity to shareholders
• Acts as a tool for capital restructuring
2. Delisting of Securities (5 Marks)
Delisting (1 Mark)
Delisting refers to the removal of a company’s securities from a
recognized stock exchange, making them no longer available for
trading on that exchange under Securities and Exchange Board of India
regulations.
Features (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Can be voluntary (by company promoters) or compulsory
• Governed by SEBI Regulations
• Requires shareholder approval and fair exit price
• Reverse book-building process often used for price discovery
•   Post-delisting, shares become illiquid
Significance (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Allows companies to go private and reduce compliance burden
• Helps in corporate restructuring and consolidation
•   Protects investor interest through exit opportunities
• Removes non-compliant or financially weak firms from
exchanges
National Housing Bank (NHB) (5 Marks)
a
Role (2 Marks)
The National Housing Bank was established in 1988 as an apex
institution for housing finance. It promotes, regulates, and develops
Q4   housing finance institutions and works under the overall supervision of
CO-1,2;   the Reserve Bank of India.   [10]
BL-2   Functions (2 Marks)
• Regulation & Supervision: Oversees housing finance
companies (now largely transferred to RBI).
• Refinance Support: Provides refinance to banks and HFCs for
housing loans.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
• Promotion of Housing Finance: Encourages new institutions
and affordable housing schemes.
•   Securitization: Develops secondary mortgage markets and
liquidity mechanisms.
Importance (1 Mark)
• Promotes housing sector growth and employment
• Enhances financial inclusion through housing finance
• Ensures stability and development of housing finance system
Association of Merchant Bankers of India (AMBI) (5 Marks)
Role (2 Marks)
The Association of Merchant Bankers of India is a professional body
representing merchant bankers in India. It functions under the
regulatory framework of the Securities and Exchange Board of India
to promote healthy practices in merchant banking.
Functions (2 Marks)
• Code of Conduct: Sets ethical standards for merchant bankers
• Industry Representation: Acts as a bridge between SEBI and
members
• Training &   Development:   Conducts   seminars   and
professional programs
• Regulatory Support: Facilitates compliance with SEBI
guidelines
Importance (1 Mark)
• Ensures efficient functioning of capital markets
• Builds investor confidence through transparency
• Promotes professionalism in merchant banking
Role of the Secondary Market in the Indian Financial System
The secondary market refers to the segment of the capital market where
existing securities are traded among investors, rather than being issued
for the first time. In India, major secondary markets include the
Bombay Stock Exchange and the National Stock Exchange, regulated
by the Securities and Exchange Board of India. It plays a crucial
supportive role in the overall financial system by enhancing efficiency,
investor confidence, and capital formation.
(10 Marks; Any Five; 2 Mark each)
CO-1, 2;   1. Promotion of Liquidity
b   [10]
BL-4   Liquidity refers to the ease with which securities can be bought or sold
without significant price changes.
• Secondary markets provide continuous trading platforms,
enabling investors to convert securities into cash quickly.
• High liquidity reduces investment risk and encourages
participation from both retail and institutional investors.
•   This, in turn, boosts primary market activity, as investors are
more willing to subscribe to new issues knowing they can exit
easily.
2. Ensuring Price Discovery
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
Price discovery is the process of determining the fair market value of
securities through demand and supply forces.
• Secondary markets facilitate real-time price formation based on
information, expectations, and trading activity.
• Transparent electronic trading systems (like NSE’s screen-
based trading) ensure efficient and unbiased pricing.
• This helps in allocating resources efficiently across sectors.
3. Facilitating Transfer of Securities
• Secondary markets enable the transfer of ownership of
securities from one investor to another.
• With the introduction of dematerialization through National
Securities Depository Limited and Central Depository Services
Limited, transfers have become:
o Faster
o Safer
o Paperless
• This reduces transaction costs and operational risks.
4. Strengthening the Capital Market
• A vibrant secondary market enhances investor confidence,
which indirectly supports the primary market.
•   It provides exit options, encouraging long-term investments in
equity.
•   It attracts foreign portfolio investors (FPIs), thereby integrating
the Indian market with global financial systems.
• Improved market depth and breadth lead to overall capital
market development.
5. Importance
While the secondary market is indispensable, some limitations exist:
•   Speculative trading can lead to volatility.
• Market manipulation risks, though controlled by SEBI, still
exist.
a   1. Suitability of DFIs over Commercial Banks (3 Marks)
Development Financial Institutions are better suited for such projects
due to the following reasons:
• Long-term Financing Orientation:
DFIs specialize in medium- and long-term lending, unlike
commercial banks that rely on short-term deposits and face
Q5   asset-liability mismatch issues.
CO-1,2;   • Developmental Mandate:   [10]
BL-2   DFIs are driven by economic and social objectives, not just
profit maximization.
• Higher Risk Appetite:
DFIs are equipped to finance projects with long gestation
periods and uncertain returns, which commercial banks
typically avoid.
2. Financial and Developmental Roles of DFIs (4 Marks)
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
DFIs play a dual role financial and developmental:
A. Financial Roles (2 Marks)
•   Project   Financing:   Provide   long-term   loans,   equity
participation, or hybrid instruments.
• Refinancing Support: Extend refinance to other institutions
involved.
• Syndication of Funds: Mobilize resources by coordinating with
multiple lenders.
B. Developmental Roles (2 Marks)
• Technical Assistance: Conduct feasibility studies, project
appraisal, and monitoring.
• Capacity Building: Support state agencies and local bodies in
project execution.
• Catalytic Role: Encourage private participation and attract
additional investment.
• Balanced Regional Development: Promote infrastructure in
underserved rural areas.
3. Risks in Long-Term Infrastructure Financing & DFI
Management (3 Marks)
Risks (Any Three;1.5 Marks)
•   Project Risk: Delays in construction, cost overruns.
•   Financial Risk: Interest rate fluctuations, funding mismatches.
•   Political/Policy Risk: Changes in government policies or
priorities.
• Demand Risk: Lower-than-expected usage (e.g., rural roads).
Risk Management Strategies (Any Three; 1.5 Marks)
• Robust Appraisal & Monitoring: Detailed techno-economic
feasibility analysis.
•   Structured Financing: Use of phased disbursements and
milestone-based funding.
• Risk Sharing: Co-financing with multilateral agencies or
government guarantees.
• Hedging & ALM Practices: Manage interest rate and maturity
risks.
•   Policy Support: Secure long-term agreements and regulatory
clearances.
The relationship between monetary policy and the money market in
b
India is both instrumental and transmission-oriented. Monetary policy,
formulated by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), operates largely
CO-1, 2;   through the money market, which acts as the immediate arena where
[10]
BL-2   policy signals are implemented and transmitted to the broader financial
system.
(10 Marks; Any Four; 2.5 Mark each)
1. Conceptual Linkage
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
• Monetary policy refers to actions by the RBI to regulate money
supply and credit conditions to achieve objectives like price
stability, growth, and financial stability.
• The money market is the market for short-term funds (maturity
up to 1 year), including instruments such as Treasury Bills,
Commercial Paper, and Call Money.
• The money market serves as the operational field for monetary
policy.
2. Transmission Mechanism
• The RBI uses policy rates (repo rate, reverse repo rate, MSF) to
influence short-term interest rates.
• These rates directly affect the call money market and other
short-term instruments.
• Changes in money market rates are transmitted to:
o Bank lending rates
o Deposit rates
o Investment and consumption decisions
Thus, the money market acts as the first channel of transmission of
monetary policy.
3. Liquidity Management Role
• The RBI manages liquidity through tools like:
o Open Market Operations
o Liquidity Adjustment Facility
• These operations are conducted in the money market to:
o   Inject liquidity
o Absorb liquidity
4. Interest Rate Determination
• Short-term interest rates in the money market are guided by RBI
policy rates.
• A well-developed money market ensures:
o Efficient price discovery
o Alignment of market rates with policy stance
Thus, monetary policy anchors the interest rate structure of the money
market.
a   The Indian Financial System (IFS) refers to the network of institutions,
markets, instruments, and services that facilitate the mobilization of
savings and their efficient allocation into productive investments. Its
structure can be understood through four core constituents:
1. Financial Institutions (3 Marks)
Q6   These are intermediaries that mobilize savings and allocate credit.
CO-1   • Banking Institutions: Commercial banks, cooperative banks,   [10]
BL-3   regional rural banks
• Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs)
• Development Financial Institutions (DFIs): e.g., NABARD,
SIDBI
• Insurance Companies & Mutual Funds
Role in Development:
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
They channel household savings into investment, provide credit to
agriculture, MSMEs, and industries, and support financial inclusion—
thus fostering capital formation and economic growth.
2. Financial Markets (2 Marks)
Markets where financial assets are traded.
• Money Market (short-term funds): Treasury Bills, Commercial
Papers
•   Capital Market (long-term funds): Equity market, debt market
Role in Development:
They ensure efficient price discovery, liquidity, and optimal allocation
of resources, enabling businesses and government to raise funds for
development projects.
3. Financial Instruments / Assets (2 Marks)
These are the claims or securities traded in financial markets.
• Primary Securities: Shares, debentures
• Secondary Securities: Bank deposits, mutual fund units
•   Derivatives: Futures, options
Role in Development:
They provide diverse investment avenues, risk-return choices, and
facilitate risk management, thereby encouraging savings and
investment.
4. Financial Services (1.5 Marks)
Services that support financial transactions and decision-making.
• Merchant banking, leasing, factoring
•   Credit rating, stock broking
• Investment advisory and wealth management
Role in Development:
They enhance efficiency, reduce transaction costs, and improve
investor confidence, leading to better functioning of financial markets.
5. Financial Regulators (1.5 Marks)
Authorities governing and supervising the system.
• RBI (banking and monetary policy)
• SEBI (capital markets)
• IRDAI (insurance)
• PFRDA (pensions)
Role in Development:
They ensure stability, transparency, and investor protection, which are
crucial for sustained economic growth.
Concept of Fee-Based Services (4 marks)
b
Fee-based services refer to financial services provided by banks and
other financial institutions where income is earned through fees,
commissions, or charges, rather than through interest on lending or
investment activities. These services are also called non-fund based
CO-1, 2;   activities, as they do not involve deployment of the institution’s own
[10]
BL-2   funds.
Features:
• Generate non-interest income
• Involve low risk compared to fund-based activities.
• Enhance customer convenience and financial intermediation
efficiency.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
• Help financial institutions reduce dependence on traditional
lending.
Types and Examples of Fee-Based Services
(6 Marks; Any Three; 2 Mark each)
1. Agency Services
Financial institutions act as agents on behalf of customers.
o Collection of cheques, dividends, interest
o Payment of insurance premiums, utility bills
o Example: Banks collecting LIC premiums or handling
ECS mandates
2. General Utility Services
Provide convenience and support services.
o Safe deposit lockers
o Issue of demand drafts, pay orders
o ATM/debit/credit card services
o Example: Annual locker rent or card issuance fees
3. Guarantee and Letter of Credit Services
Banks provide guarantees or LCs for trade and business
transactions.
o Earn commission without lending funds directly
o Example: Bank guarantee issued for a contractor
a   The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) and the Securities and Exchange
Board of India (SEBI) are two apex regulatory institutions in the Indian
financial system. While RBI governs the monetary and banking
system, SEBI regulates the capital markets and the investor protection
framework.
1. Objectives (2 Marks)
• RBI:
o To maintain monetary stability and control inflation.
o To ensure financial stability and smooth functioning of
the banking system.
Q7   • SEBI:
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-3   o To protect investor interests.
o To promote the development and regulation of securities
markets.
2. Functions (3 Marks)
• RBI Functions:
o Issue of currency notes.
o Banker to the Government and banks.
o Controller of credit through monetary policy tools
(CRR, SLR, repo rate).
o Custodian of foreign exchange reserves.
• SEBI Functions:
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---
o Regulates stock exchanges and intermediaries (brokers,
mutual funds).
o Registers and supervises market participants.
o Prevents fraudulent and unfair trade practices.
o Regulates IPOs and capital raising activities.
3. Powers (2 Marks)
• RBI Powers:
o Can regulate and supervise banks.
o Authority to frame monetary policy and issue directives
to banks.
o Can impose penalties and restrict banking operations.
• SEBI Powers:
o Can investigate and penalize market misconduct.
o Power to ban entities from trading in securities markets.
o Authority to regulate listing, trading, and disclosure
requirements.
4. Role in the Indian Financial System (3 Marks)
• RBI Role:
o Acts as the central bank, ensuring liquidity and systemic
stability.
o Maintains confidence in the currency and banking
system.
o Plays a developmental role in financial inclusion.
• SEBI Role:
o Ensures fair, transparent, and efficient capital markets.
o Builds investor confidence through regulation and
disclosure norms.
o   Facilitates capital formation and market development.
Process of Formation of SHGs (6 Marks)
b
The formation of Self-Help Groups (SHGs) is a systematic and
participatory process aimed at promoting financial inclusion and
grassroots empowerment, particularly among weaker sections.
Step-wise Process:
1. Identification of Members
•   Typically consists of 10–20 individuals (preferably from
similar socio-economic backgrounds).
CO-1, 2;   • Members are usually homogeneous (e.g., women, farmers,
[10]
BL-3   labourers).
• Emphasis is on mutual trust and shared objectives.
2. Group Formation and Orientation
•   Facilitated by NGOs, banks, or government agencies (e.g.,
NABARD initiatives).
• Members are educated about:
o Objectives of SHGs
o Benefits of savings and credit discipline
o Group norms and responsibilities
********** 14 **********
--- page 15 ---
3. Framing of Rules and Regulations
• SHG develops its own bylaws, including:
o Meeting frequency (weekly/monthly)
o Savings contribution amount
o Loan terms and interest rates
• Promotes democratic decision-making.
4. Regular Savings Mobilization
• Each member contributes a fixed small amount regularly.
• Funds are pooled to create a common corpus.
5. Internal Lending
• SHG begins lending to members from pooled savings.
• Loans are given for:
o Consumption needs
o Income-generating activities
• Builds credit discipline and financial literacy.
6. Bank Linkage
•   After demonstrating stability (usually 6 months):
o SHG opens a bank account
o Becomes eligible for bank credit (SHG-Bank Linkage
Programme)
• Enhances access to formal financial services.
7. Capacity Building and Grading
• Performance is periodically assessed.
• Training is provided for:
o Bookkeeping
o Financial management
o Entrepreneurship
Types of any two SHGs in details
(4 Marks; Any Two; 2 Mark each)
1. Economic SHGs
2. Self-Managed SHGs
********** 15 **********
````
