Q1   [20]
Definition of Financial Inclusion (1 mark)   [05]
a.
Financial inclusion refers to the process of ensuring access to
appropriate, affordable, and timely financial services such as banking,
credit, insurance, and payment services to all sections of society,
particularly the vulnerable and low-income groups, at a reasonable cost
in a transparent manner.
Initiatives to Enlarge Financial Inclusion in India
(4 Marks; Any Four; 1 mark each)
1. Banking Outreach & No-Frills Accounts
o Simplified accounts with zero or minimum balance
requirements to bring unbanked people into the formal
CO-1,2;   system.
BL-2   2. Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY)
o Massive financial inclusion programme (2014) focusing
on universal banking access, RuPay cards, overdraft
facilities, and direct benefit transfers (DBT).
3. Business Correspondent (BC) Model
o Use of agents to provide banking services in remote and
rural areas, reducing the need for physical bank
branches.
4. Digital Payment Initiatives
o Introduction of UPI, AEPS, mobile banking, and RuPay
cards to promote cashless and accessible financial
transactions.
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
5. Direct Benefit Transfer
o Transfer   of government   subsidies   directly   into
beneficiaries’ bank accounts, improving transparency
and inclusion.
6. Microfinance & Self-Help Groups (SHGs)
o Provision of small loans to low-income individuals,
especially women,   through SHG-Bank   linkage
programmes.
7. Financial Literacy and Credit Counselling
o Efforts by RBI and other institutions to educate people
about financial products and responsible usage.
A firm should prefer recourse factoring over non-recourse factoring   [05]
in situations where cost minimisation and stronger control over credit
risk are important. Under recourse factoring, the firm retains the risk
of bad debts, making it cheaper than non-recourse factoring.
Situations: (Any two; 2.5 marks each)
1. When the firm has strong credit control systems
If the firm is confident about its customers’ creditworthiness
and has efficient receivables management, it can bear the credit
risk and opt for the cheaper recourse option.
2. When   the   incidence   of   bad   debts   is   low
Firms dealing with reliable, long-term customers or operating
CO-1,2 ;   in stable industries face minimal default risk, making recourse
b.
BL-1   factoring more suitable.
3. When   cost   considerations   are   critical
Recourse factoring involves lower factoring charges since the
factor does not assume credit risk, helping firms reduce
financing costs.
4. When the firm wants only financing and collection services
If the firm does not require credit risk protection but only
liquidity and receivables management, recourse factoring is
appropriate.
5. When the firm is willing to retain risk for higher returns
By bearing the risk, firms avoid higher fees and can improve
profitability compared to non-recourse arrangements.
CO-1; BL-   Major Challenges of the Indian Financial System   [05]
1   c.
(5 Marks; Any Five; 1 mark each)
1. High   Level   of   Non-Performing   Assets   (NPAs)
Indian banks, particularly public sector banks, have historically
faced high NPAs, which have weakened profitability, reduced
lending capacity, and affected financial stability.
2. Financial   Exclusion
Despite initiatives like Jan Dhan Yojana, a significant portion
of the population still lacks access to formal financial services,
especially in rural and informal sectors.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
3. Inefficient   Financial   Intermediation
The transmission of monetary policy is often weak due to rigid
interest rates, structural bottlenecks, and dominance of banks
over capital markets.
4. Underdeveloped   Capital   Markets
Compared to advanced economies, India’s corporate bond
market remains shallow, limiting long-term financing options
for infrastructure and industry.
5. Regulatory   and   Structural   Issues
Multiplicity of regulators (RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, etc.) and
coordination challenges can lead to regulatory overlaps and
gaps.
Mortgage (2 marks)   [05]
d.
A mortgage is the transfer of an interest in specific immovable property
(such as land or building) by a borrower (mortgagor) to a lender
(mortgagee) as security for a loan. The ownership is not fully
transferred; only a limited interest is created to secure repayment.
Upon repayment of the loan, the interest is extinguished, and the
property reverts fully to the borrower.
One Mortgage Instrument (3 marks):
A simple mortgage is one of the most common forms of mortgage in
India.
CO-1, 2 ;   Features:
BL-2   1. The mortgagor does not deliver possession of the property to
the lender.
2. The borrower personally binds himself to repay the loan.
3. In case of default, the mortgagee has the right to cause the
property to be sold through legal process.
4. The lender cannot take possession but can approach the court
for recovery.
Example:
A person takes a housing loan from a bank and creates a charge on their
house as security. If he fails to repay, the bank can sell the property
through legal means.
a   Debt Instruments (5 marks)
Meaning:
Debt instruments represent a contractual obligation whereby the issuer
(borrower) promises to pay periodic interest and repay the principal to
the investor (lender).
Q2   Types in India:
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-2   • Government Securities (G-Secs): Issued by Central/State
Governments
• Corporate Bonds/Debentures: Issued by companies
• Money Market Instruments: Treasury Bills, Commercial
Paper (CP), Certificates of Deposit (CD)
Features:
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
• Fixed income (interest-bearing)
• Defined maturity
• Lower risk (especially G-Secs)
Role in the Indian Financial System:
• Mobilization of savings: Channels household and institutional
savings into productive uses
• Financing fiscal deficit: Government relies on G-Secs
• Benchmark for interest rates: G-Sec yields act as a reference
for pricing other instruments
• Liquidity management: Used by RBI in Open Market
Operations (OMO)
Derivatives (5 marks)
Meaning:
Derivatives are financial contracts whose value is derived from an
underlying asset such as equity, interest rates, currency, or
commodities.
Types in India:
•   Futures and Options (Equity & Index derivatives)
• Currency derivatives
•   Interest Rate Futures (IRFs)
Features:
• No initial full investment (margin-based trading)
• Used for hedging, speculation, and arbitrage
• High leverage
Role in the Indian Financial System:
• Risk management: Helps firms hedge against price, interest
rate, and exchange rate risks
• Price discovery: Reflects market expectations about future
prices
• Market efficiency: Enhances liquidity and depth of financial
markets
• Integration with global markets: Aligns Indian markets with
international practices
b   1. Venture Capital (1 Mark)
Venture Capital (VC) refers to risk capital provided to high-growth,
innovative Startups and small businesses with long-term potential. It is
typically equity-based financing, where investors participate in both
risk and returns.
2. Role of Venture Capital in the Indian Financial System
CO-1,2;
(3 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each) [10]
BL-3
Venture capital plays a pivotal role in strengthening India’s financial
architecture:
• Bridging Financing Gap: Provides funds to startups lacking
access to traditional sources like banks or capital markets.
• Promoting Innovation: Encourages technology-driven and
knowledge-based industries (e.g., fintech, biotech).
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
• Employment Generation: Supports startups that create jobs
and boost economic development.
• Capital Market Development: Successful VC-backed firms
often go for IPOs, deepening capital markets.
• Risk Diversification: Channels funds into high-risk, high-
return ventures, improving allocation efficiency.
3. Importance for Entrepreneurial Growth
(3 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each)
Venture capital is crucial for entrepreneurship due to the following
reasons:
• Access to Risk Capital: Entrepreneurs often lack collateral;
VC provides funding without requiring security.
• Managerial & Technical Support: VC firms offer mentorship,
governance, and strategic guidance.
• Market Credibility: Association with reputed VC firms
enhances trust among stakeholders.
• Long-Term Orientation: Unlike banks, VC investors are
patient and focus on growth rather than immediate returns.
• Encouragement of Innovation Ecosystem: Supports Startups
in emerging sectors, fostering a culture of innovation in India.
4. Stages of Venture Capital Financing (3 Marks)
Venture capital financing typically proceeds through the following
stages:
(i) Seed Stage
•   Initial funding for idea development, research, and prototype
creation.
• High risk, as the business model is not yet proven.
(ii) Start-up Stage
• Financing for product development and initial marketing.
• The firm begins operations but may not generate profits.
(iii) Early Growth Stage (First Stage Financing)
• Funds used for scaling production and expanding market reach.
• Company starts generating revenue.
(iv) Expansion Stage (Second/Third Stage Financing)
•   Capital for expansion, diversification, and increasing capacity.
• Risk reduces as the business stabilizes.
(v) Exit Stage
• VC investors exit through IPO, mergers, or acquisitions.
•   Realization of returns on investment.
An Initial Public Offer (IPO) is the first sale of securities by a company
a   to the public in the primary market. The investment banker (also called
merchant banker) plays a central role in managing, structuring, and
Q3
CO-1, 2;   executing the issue.   [10]
BL-3   1. Role of Investment Banker in IPO
(4 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each)
As an investment banker, I would assist the company in the following
ways:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
(a) Issue Planning & Structuring
• Assess capital requirements and decide issue size, pricing, and
timing
• Choose between fixed price issue or book-building method
(b) Due Diligence
• Conduct financial, legal, and business verification
• Ensure compliance with Securities and Exchange Board of
India regulations
(c) Drafting Offer Document
• Prepare Draft Red Herring Prospectus (DRHP)
•   Disclose all material information for investor protection
(d) Underwriting & Syndication
• Arrange underwriters to guarantee subscription
• Appoint intermediaries: brokers, registrars, bankers
(e) Marketing the Issue
• Conduct roadshows, investor meetings, and advertisements
• Build investor confidence and demand
2. Steps in Public Issue Process (6 Marks)
The IPO process involves the following sequential steps:
(1) Appointment of Intermediaries
• Merchant banker, registrar, underwriters, legal advisors are
appointed
(2) Preparation & Filing of DRHP
•   Draft prospectus submitted to SEBI for approval
• SEBI reviews disclosures and compliance
(3) SEBI Approval
• Observations given by SEBI
• Company incorporates required changes
(4) Pricing of Issue
•   Final price determined:
o Fixed price OR
o Book building process (price discovery through bids)
(5) Marketing & Roadshows
• Promotion through institutional and retail investor outreach
(6) Opening of IPO
•   Public subscription opens for a fixed period (usually 3–5 days)
(7) Allotment of Shares
•   Basis of allotment finalized (oversubscription handled
proportionately)
(8) Listing on Stock Exchange
• Shares listed on exchanges like the National Stock Exchange or
Bombay Stock Exchange
• Trading begins in the secondary market
CO-1,2 ;   b   Buyback of Shares (5 Marks)
[10]
BL-2   Meaning (1 Mark)
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Buyback of shares refers to the process by which a company
repurchases   its own shares from existing shareholders at a
predetermined price, as per regulations laid down by the Securities and
Exchange Board of India.
Features (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Can be carried out through tender offer, open market purchase,
or book-building process
• Governed by SEBI (Buyback of Securities) Regulations
• Leads to a reduction in share capital
• Improves earnings per share (EPS) and return ratios
•   Subject to limits on debt-equity ratio and quantum of buyback
Significance (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
•   Signals confidence of management in company prospects
• Helps in price stabilization during market volatility
• Provides an exit opportunity to shareholders
• Acts as a tool for capital restructuring
2. Delisting of Securities (5 Marks)
Delisting (1 Mark)
Delisting refers to the removal of a company’s securities from a
recognized stock exchange, making them no longer available for
trading on that exchange under Securities and Exchange Board of India
regulations.
Features (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Can be voluntary (by company promoters) or compulsory
• Governed by SEBI Regulations
• Requires shareholder approval and fair exit price
• Reverse book-building process often used for price discovery
•   Post-delisting, shares become illiquid
Significance (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Allows companies to go private and reduce compliance burden
• Helps in corporate restructuring and consolidation
•   Protects investor interest through exit opportunities
• Removes non-compliant or financially weak firms from
exchanges
National Housing Bank (NHB) (5 Marks)
a
Role (2 Marks)
The National Housing Bank was established in 1988 as an apex
institution for housing finance. It promotes, regulates, and develops
Q4   housing finance institutions and works under the overall supervision of
CO-1,2;   the Reserve Bank of India.   [10]
BL-2   Functions (2 Marks)
• Regulation & Supervision: Oversees housing finance
companies (now largely transferred to RBI).
• Refinance Support: Provides refinance to banks and HFCs for
housing loans.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
• Promotion of Housing Finance: Encourages new institutions
and affordable housing schemes.
•   Securitization: Develops secondary mortgage markets and
liquidity mechanisms.
Importance (1 Mark)
• Promotes housing sector growth and employment
• Enhances financial inclusion through housing finance
• Ensures stability and development of housing finance system
Association of Merchant Bankers of India (AMBI) (5 Marks)
Role (2 Marks)
The Association of Merchant Bankers of India is a professional body
representing merchant bankers in India. It functions under the
regulatory framework of the Securities and Exchange Board of India
to promote healthy practices in merchant banking.
Functions (2 Marks)
• Code of Conduct: Sets ethical standards for merchant bankers
• Industry Representation: Acts as a bridge between SEBI and
members
• Training &   Development:   Conducts   seminars   and
professional programs
• Regulatory Support: Facilitates compliance with SEBI
guidelines
Importance (1 Mark)
• Ensures efficient functioning of capital markets
• Builds investor confidence through transparency
• Promotes professionalism in merchant banking
Role of the Secondary Market in the Indian Financial System
The secondary market refers to the segment of the capital market where
existing securities are traded among investors, rather than being issued
for the first time. In India, major secondary markets include the
Bombay Stock Exchange and the National Stock Exchange, regulated
by the Securities and Exchange Board of India. It plays a crucial
supportive role in the overall financial system by enhancing efficiency,
investor confidence, and capital formation.
(10 Marks; Any Five; 2 Mark each)
CO-1, 2;   1. Promotion of Liquidity
b   [10]
BL-4   Liquidity refers to the ease with which securities can be bought or sold
without significant price changes.
• Secondary markets provide continuous trading platforms,
enabling investors to convert securities into cash quickly.
• High liquidity reduces investment risk and encourages
participation from both retail and institutional investors.
•   This, in turn, boosts primary market activity, as investors are
more willing to subscribe to new issues knowing they can exit
easily.
2. Ensuring Price Discovery
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
Price discovery is the process of determining the fair market value of
securities through demand and supply forces.
• Secondary markets facilitate real-time price formation based on
information, expectations, and trading activity.
• Transparent electronic trading systems (like NSE’s screen-
based trading) ensure efficient and unbiased pricing.
• This helps in allocating resources efficiently across sectors.
3. Facilitating Transfer of Securities
• Secondary markets enable the transfer of ownership of
securities from one investor to another.
• With the introduction of dematerialization through National
Securities Depository Limited and Central Depository Services
Limited, transfers have become:
o Faster
o Safer
o Paperless
• This reduces transaction costs and operational risks.
4. Strengthening the Capital Market
• A vibrant secondary market enhances investor confidence,
which indirectly supports the primary market.
•   It provides exit options, encouraging long-term investments in
equity.
•   It attracts foreign portfolio investors (FPIs), thereby integrating
the Indian market with global financial systems.
• Improved market depth and breadth lead to overall capital
market development.
5. Importance
While the secondary market is indispensable, some limitations exist:
•   Speculative trading can lead to volatility.
• Market manipulation risks, though controlled by SEBI, still
exist.
a   1. Suitability of DFIs over Commercial Banks (3 Marks)
Development Financial Institutions are better suited for such projects
due to the following reasons:
• Long-term Financing Orientation:
DFIs specialize in medium- and long-term lending, unlike
commercial banks that rely on short-term deposits and face
Q5   asset-liability mismatch issues.
CO-1,2;   • Developmental Mandate:   [10]
BL-2   DFIs are driven by economic and social objectives, not just
profit maximization.
• Higher Risk Appetite:
DFIs are equipped to finance projects with long gestation
periods and uncertain returns, which commercial banks
typically avoid.
2. Financial and Developmental Roles of DFIs (4 Marks)
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
DFIs play a dual role financial and developmental:
A. Financial Roles (2 Marks)
•   Project   Financing:   Provide   long-term   loans,   equity
participation, or hybrid instruments.
• Refinancing Support: Extend refinance to other institutions
involved.
• Syndication of Funds: Mobilize resources by coordinating with
multiple lenders.
B. Developmental Roles (2 Marks)
• Technical Assistance: Conduct feasibility studies, project
appraisal, and monitoring.
• Capacity Building: Support state agencies and local bodies in
project execution.
• Catalytic Role: Encourage private participation and attract
additional investment.
• Balanced Regional Development: Promote infrastructure in
underserved rural areas.
3. Risks in Long-Term Infrastructure Financing & DFI
Management (3 Marks)
Risks (Any Three;1.5 Marks)
•   Project Risk: Delays in construction, cost overruns.
•   Financial Risk: Interest rate fluctuations, funding mismatches.
•   Political/Policy Risk: Changes in government policies or
priorities.
• Demand Risk: Lower-than-expected usage (e.g., rural roads).
Risk Management Strategies (Any Three; 1.5 Marks)
• Robust Appraisal & Monitoring: Detailed techno-economic
feasibility analysis.
•   Structured Financing: Use of phased disbursements and
milestone-based funding.
• Risk Sharing: Co-financing with multilateral agencies or
government guarantees.
• Hedging & ALM Practices: Manage interest rate and maturity
risks.
•   Policy Support: Secure long-term agreements and regulatory
clearances.
The relationship between monetary policy and the money market in
b
India is both instrumental and transmission-oriented. Monetary policy,
formulated by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), operates largely
CO-1, 2;   through the money market, which acts as the immediate arena where
[10]
BL-2   policy signals are implemented and transmitted to the broader financial
system.
(10 Marks; Any Four; 2.5 Mark each)
1. Conceptual Linkage
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
• Monetary policy refers to actions by the RBI to regulate money
supply and credit conditions to achieve objectives like price
stability, growth, and financial stability.
• The money market is the market for short-term funds (maturity
up to 1 year), including instruments such as Treasury Bills,
Commercial Paper, and Call Money.
• The money market serves as the operational field for monetary
policy.
2. Transmission Mechanism
• The RBI uses policy rates (repo rate, reverse repo rate, MSF) to
influence short-term interest rates.
• These rates directly affect the call money market and other
short-term instruments.
• Changes in money market rates are transmitted to:
o Bank lending rates
o Deposit rates
o Investment and consumption decisions
Thus, the money market acts as the first channel of transmission of
monetary policy.
3. Liquidity Management Role
• The RBI manages liquidity through tools like:
o Open Market Operations
o Liquidity Adjustment Facility
• These operations are conducted in the money market to:
o   Inject liquidity
o Absorb liquidity
4. Interest Rate Determination
• Short-term interest rates in the money market are guided by RBI
policy rates.
• A well-developed money market ensures:
o Efficient price discovery
o Alignment of market rates with policy stance
Thus, monetary policy anchors the interest rate structure of the money
market.
a   The Indian Financial System (IFS) refers to the network of institutions,
markets, instruments, and services that facilitate the mobilization of
savings and their efficient allocation into productive investments. Its
structure can be understood through four core constituents:
1. Financial Institutions (3 Marks)
Q6   These are intermediaries that mobilize savings and allocate credit.
CO-1   • Banking Institutions: Commercial banks, cooperative banks,   [10]
BL-3   regional rural banks
• Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs)
• Development Financial Institutions (DFIs): e.g., NABARD,
SIDBI
• Insurance Companies & Mutual Funds
Role in Development:
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
They channel household savings into investment, provide credit to
agriculture, MSMEs, and industries, and support financial inclusion—
thus fostering capital formation and economic growth.
2. Financial Markets (2 Marks)
Markets where financial assets are traded.
• Money Market (short-term funds): Treasury Bills, Commercial
Papers
•   Capital Market (long-term funds): Equity market, debt market
Role in Development:
They ensure efficient price discovery, liquidity, and optimal allocation
of resources, enabling businesses and government to raise funds for
development projects.
3. Financial Instruments / Assets (2 Marks)
These are the claims or securities traded in financial markets.
• Primary Securities: Shares, debentures
• Secondary Securities: Bank deposits, mutual fund units
•   Derivatives: Futures, options
Role in Development:
They provide diverse investment avenues, risk-return choices, and
facilitate risk management, thereby encouraging savings and
investment.
4. Financial Services (1.5 Marks)
Services that support financial transactions and decision-making.
• Merchant banking, leasing, factoring
•   Credit rating, stock broking
• Investment advisory and wealth management
Role in Development:
They enhance efficiency, reduce transaction costs, and improve
investor confidence, leading to better functioning of financial markets.
5. Financial Regulators (1.5 Marks)
Authorities governing and supervising the system.
• RBI (banking and monetary policy)
• SEBI (capital markets)
• IRDAI (insurance)
• PFRDA (pensions)
Role in Development:
They ensure stability, transparency, and investor protection, which are
crucial for sustained economic growth.
Concept of Fee-Based Services (4 marks)
b
Fee-based services refer to financial services provided by banks and
other financial institutions where income is earned through fees,
commissions, or charges, rather than through interest on lending or
investment activities. These services are also called non-fund based
CO-1, 2;   activities, as they do not involve deployment of the institution’s own
[10]
BL-2   funds.
Features:
• Generate non-interest income
• Involve low risk compared to fund-based activities.
• Enhance customer convenience and financial intermediation
efficiency.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
• Help financial institutions reduce dependence on traditional
lending.
Types and Examples of Fee-Based Services
(6 Marks; Any Three; 2 Mark each)
1. Agency Services
Financial institutions act as agents on behalf of customers.
o Collection of cheques, dividends, interest
o Payment of insurance premiums, utility bills
o Example: Banks collecting LIC premiums or handling
ECS mandates
2. General Utility Services
Provide convenience and support services.
o Safe deposit lockers
o Issue of demand drafts, pay orders
o ATM/debit/credit card services
o Example: Annual locker rent or card issuance fees
3. Guarantee and Letter of Credit Services
Banks provide guarantees or LCs for trade and business
transactions.
o Earn commission without lending funds directly
o Example: Bank guarantee issued for a contractor
a   The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) and the Securities and Exchange
Board of India (SEBI) are two apex regulatory institutions in the Indian
financial system. While RBI governs the monetary and banking
system, SEBI regulates the capital markets and the investor protection
framework.
1. Objectives (2 Marks)
• RBI:
o To maintain monetary stability and control inflation.
o To ensure financial stability and smooth functioning of
the banking system.
Q7   • SEBI:
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-3   o To protect investor interests.
o To promote the development and regulation of securities
markets.
2. Functions (3 Marks)
• RBI Functions:
o Issue of currency notes.
o Banker to the Government and banks.
o Controller of credit through monetary policy tools
(CRR, SLR, repo rate).
o Custodian of foreign exchange reserves.
• SEBI Functions:
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---
o Regulates stock exchanges and intermediaries (brokers,
mutual funds).
o Registers and supervises market participants.
o Prevents fraudulent and unfair trade practices.
o Regulates IPOs and capital raising activities.
3. Powers (2 Marks)
• RBI Powers:
o Can regulate and supervise banks.
o Authority to frame monetary policy and issue directives
to banks.
o Can impose penalties and restrict banking operations.
• SEBI Powers:
o Can investigate and penalize market misconduct.
o Power to ban entities from trading in securities markets.
o Authority to regulate listing, trading, and disclosure
requirements.
4. Role in the Indian Financial System (3 Marks)
• RBI Role:
o Acts as the central bank, ensuring liquidity and systemic
stability.
o Maintains confidence in the currency and banking
system.
o Plays a developmental role in financial inclusion.
• SEBI Role:
o Ensures fair, transparent, and efficient capital markets.
o Builds investor confidence through regulation and
disclosure norms.
o   Facilitates capital formation and market development.
Process of Formation of SHGs (6 Marks)
b
The formation of Self-Help Groups (SHGs) is a systematic and
participatory process aimed at promoting financial inclusion and
grassroots empowerment, particularly among weaker sections.
Step-wise Process:
1. Identification of Members
•   Typically consists of 10–20 individuals (preferably from
similar socio-economic backgrounds).
CO-1, 2;   • Members are usually homogeneous (e.g., women, farmers,
[10]
BL-3   labourers).
• Emphasis is on mutual trust and shared objectives.
2. Group Formation and Orientation
•   Facilitated by NGOs, banks, or government agencies (e.g.,
NABARD initiatives).
• Members are educated about:
o Objectives of SHGs
o Benefits of savings and credit discipline
o Group norms and responsibilities
********** 14 **********
--- page 15 ---
3. Framing of Rules and Regulations
• SHG develops its own bylaws, including:
o Meeting frequency (weekly/monthly)
o Savings contribution amount
o Loan terms and interest rates
• Promotes democratic decision-making.
4. Regular Savings Mobilization
• Each member contributes a fixed small amount regularly.
• Funds are pooled to create a common corpus.
5. Internal Lending
• SHG begins lending to members from pooled savings.
• Loans are given for:
o Consumption needs
o Income-generating activities
• Builds credit discipline and financial literacy.
6. Bank Linkage
•   After demonstrating stability (usually 6 months):
o SHG opens a bank account
o Becomes eligible for bank credit (SHG-Bank Linkage
Programme)
• Enhances access to formal financial services.
7. Capacity Building and Grading
• Performance is periodically assessed.
• Training is provided for:
o Bookkeeping
o Financial management
o Entrepreneurship
Types of any two SHGs in details
(4 Marks; Any Two; 2 Mark each)
1. Economic SHGs
2. Self-Managed SHGs
********** 15 **********
````

Q1   [20]
Definition of Financial Inclusion (1 mark)   [05]
a.
Financial inclusion refers to the process of ensuring access to
appropriate, affordable, and timely financial services such as banking,
credit, insurance, and payment services to all sections of society,
particularly the vulnerable and low-income groups, at a reasonable cost
in a transparent manner.
Initiatives to Enlarge Financial Inclusion in India
(4 Marks; Any Four; 1 mark each)
1. Banking Outreach & No-Frills Accounts
o Simplified accounts with zero or minimum balance
requirements to bring unbanked people into the formal
CO-1,2;   system.
BL-2   2. Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY)
o Massive financial inclusion programme (2014) focusing
on universal banking access, RuPay cards, overdraft
facilities, and direct benefit transfers (DBT).
3. Business Correspondent (BC) Model
o Use of agents to provide banking services in remote and
rural areas, reducing the need for physical bank
branches.
4. Digital Payment Initiatives
o Introduction of UPI, AEPS, mobile banking, and RuPay
cards to promote cashless and accessible financial
transactions.
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
5. Direct Benefit Transfer
o Transfer   of government   subsidies   directly   into
beneficiaries’ bank accounts, improving transparency
and inclusion.
6. Microfinance & Self-Help Groups (SHGs)
o Provision of small loans to low-income individuals,
especially women,   through SHG-Bank   linkage
programmes.
7. Financial Literacy and Credit Counselling
o Efforts by RBI and other institutions to educate people
about financial products and responsible usage.
A firm should prefer recourse factoring over non-recourse factoring   [05]
in situations where cost minimisation and stronger control over credit
risk are important. Under recourse factoring, the firm retains the risk
of bad debts, making it cheaper than non-recourse factoring.
Situations: (Any two; 2.5 marks each)
1. When the firm has strong credit control systems
If the firm is confident about its customers’ creditworthiness
and has efficient receivables management, it can bear the credit
risk and opt for the cheaper recourse option.
2. When   the   incidence   of   bad   debts   is   low
Firms dealing with reliable, long-term customers or operating
CO-1,2 ;   in stable industries face minimal default risk, making recourse
b.
BL-1   factoring more suitable.
3. When   cost   considerations   are   critical
Recourse factoring involves lower factoring charges since the
factor does not assume credit risk, helping firms reduce
financing costs.
4. When the firm wants only financing and collection services
If the firm does not require credit risk protection but only
liquidity and receivables management, recourse factoring is
appropriate.
5. When the firm is willing to retain risk for higher returns
By bearing the risk, firms avoid higher fees and can improve
profitability compared to non-recourse arrangements.
CO-1; BL-   Major Challenges of the Indian Financial System   [05]
1   c.
(5 Marks; Any Five; 1 mark each)
1. High   Level   of   Non-Performing   Assets   (NPAs)
Indian banks, particularly public sector banks, have historically
faced high NPAs, which have weakened profitability, reduced
lending capacity, and affected financial stability.
2. Financial   Exclusion
Despite initiatives like Jan Dhan Yojana, a significant portion
of the population still lacks access to formal financial services,
especially in rural and informal sectors.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
3. Inefficient   Financial   Intermediation
The transmission of monetary policy is often weak due to rigid
interest rates, structural bottlenecks, and dominance of banks
over capital markets.
4. Underdeveloped   Capital   Markets
Compared to advanced economies, India’s corporate bond
market remains shallow, limiting long-term financing options
for infrastructure and industry.
5. Regulatory   and   Structural   Issues
Multiplicity of regulators (RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, etc.) and
coordination challenges can lead to regulatory overlaps and
gaps.
Mortgage (2 marks)   [05]
d.
A mortgage is the transfer of an interest in specific immovable property
(such as land or building) by a borrower (mortgagor) to a lender
(mortgagee) as security for a loan. The ownership is not fully
transferred; only a limited interest is created to secure repayment.
Upon repayment of the loan, the interest is extinguished, and the
property reverts fully to the borrower.
One Mortgage Instrument (3 marks):
A simple mortgage is one of the most common forms of mortgage in
India.
CO-1, 2 ;   Features:
BL-2   1. The mortgagor does not deliver possession of the property to
the lender.
2. The borrower personally binds himself to repay the loan.
3. In case of default, the mortgagee has the right to cause the
property to be sold through legal process.
4. The lender cannot take possession but can approach the court
for recovery.
Example:
A person takes a housing loan from a bank and creates a charge on their
house as security. If he fails to repay, the bank can sell the property
through legal means.
a   Debt Instruments (5 marks)
Meaning:
Debt instruments represent a contractual obligation whereby the issuer
(borrower) promises to pay periodic interest and repay the principal to
the investor (lender).
Q2   Types in India:
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-2   • Government Securities (G-Secs): Issued by Central/State
Governments
• Corporate Bonds/Debentures: Issued by companies
• Money Market Instruments: Treasury Bills, Commercial
Paper (CP), Certificates of Deposit (CD)
Features:
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
• Fixed income (interest-bearing)
• Defined maturity
• Lower risk (especially G-Secs)
Role in the Indian Financial System:
• Mobilization of savings: Channels household and institutional
savings into productive uses
• Financing fiscal deficit: Government relies on G-Secs
• Benchmark for interest rates: G-Sec yields act as a reference
for pricing other instruments
• Liquidity management: Used by RBI in Open Market
Operations (OMO)
Derivatives (5 marks)
Meaning:
Derivatives are financial contracts whose value is derived from an
underlying asset such as equity, interest rates, currency, or
commodities.
Types in India:
•   Futures and Options (Equity & Index derivatives)
• Currency derivatives
•   Interest Rate Futures (IRFs)
Features:
• No initial full investment (margin-based trading)
• Used for hedging, speculation, and arbitrage
• High leverage
Role in the Indian Financial System:
• Risk management: Helps firms hedge against price, interest
rate, and exchange rate risks
• Price discovery: Reflects market expectations about future
prices
• Market efficiency: Enhances liquidity and depth of financial
markets
• Integration with global markets: Aligns Indian markets with
international practices
b   1. Venture Capital (1 Mark)
Venture Capital (VC) refers to risk capital provided to high-growth,
innovative Startups and small businesses with long-term potential. It is
typically equity-based financing, where investors participate in both
risk and returns.
2. Role of Venture Capital in the Indian Financial System
CO-1,2;
(3 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each) [10]
BL-3
Venture capital plays a pivotal role in strengthening India’s financial
architecture:
• Bridging Financing Gap: Provides funds to startups lacking
access to traditional sources like banks or capital markets.
• Promoting Innovation: Encourages technology-driven and
knowledge-based industries (e.g., fintech, biotech).
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
• Employment Generation: Supports startups that create jobs
and boost economic development.
• Capital Market Development: Successful VC-backed firms
often go for IPOs, deepening capital markets.
• Risk Diversification: Channels funds into high-risk, high-
return ventures, improving allocation efficiency.
3. Importance for Entrepreneurial Growth
(3 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each)
Venture capital is crucial for entrepreneurship due to the following
reasons:
• Access to Risk Capital: Entrepreneurs often lack collateral;
VC provides funding without requiring security.
• Managerial & Technical Support: VC firms offer mentorship,
governance, and strategic guidance.
• Market Credibility: Association with reputed VC firms
enhances trust among stakeholders.
• Long-Term Orientation: Unlike banks, VC investors are
patient and focus on growth rather than immediate returns.
• Encouragement of Innovation Ecosystem: Supports Startups
in emerging sectors, fostering a culture of innovation in India.
4. Stages of Venture Capital Financing (3 Marks)
Venture capital financing typically proceeds through the following
stages:
(i) Seed Stage
•   Initial funding for idea development, research, and prototype
creation.
• High risk, as the business model is not yet proven.
(ii) Start-up Stage
• Financing for product development and initial marketing.
• The firm begins operations but may not generate profits.
(iii) Early Growth Stage (First Stage Financing)
• Funds used for scaling production and expanding market reach.
• Company starts generating revenue.
(iv) Expansion Stage (Second/Third Stage Financing)
•   Capital for expansion, diversification, and increasing capacity.
• Risk reduces as the business stabilizes.
(v) Exit Stage
• VC investors exit through IPO, mergers, or acquisitions.
•   Realization of returns on investment.
An Initial Public Offer (IPO) is the first sale of securities by a company
a   to the public in the primary market. The investment banker (also called
merchant banker) plays a central role in managing, structuring, and
Q3
CO-1, 2;   executing the issue.   [10]
BL-3   1. Role of Investment Banker in IPO
(4 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each)
As an investment banker, I would assist the company in the following
ways:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
(a) Issue Planning & Structuring
• Assess capital requirements and decide issue size, pricing, and
timing
• Choose between fixed price issue or book-building method
(b) Due Diligence
• Conduct financial, legal, and business verification
• Ensure compliance with Securities and Exchange Board of
India regulations
(c) Drafting Offer Document
• Prepare Draft Red Herring Prospectus (DRHP)
•   Disclose all material information for investor protection
(d) Underwriting & Syndication
• Arrange underwriters to guarantee subscription
• Appoint intermediaries: brokers, registrars, bankers
(e) Marketing the Issue
• Conduct roadshows, investor meetings, and advertisements
• Build investor confidence and demand
2. Steps in Public Issue Process (6 Marks)
The IPO process involves the following sequential steps:
(1) Appointment of Intermediaries
• Merchant banker, registrar, underwriters, legal advisors are
appointed
(2) Preparation & Filing of DRHP
•   Draft prospectus submitted to SEBI for approval
• SEBI reviews disclosures and compliance
(3) SEBI Approval
• Observations given by SEBI
• Company incorporates required changes
(4) Pricing of Issue
•   Final price determined:
o Fixed price OR
o Book building process (price discovery through bids)
(5) Marketing & Roadshows
• Promotion through institutional and retail investor outreach
(6) Opening of IPO
•   Public subscription opens for a fixed period (usually 3–5 days)
(7) Allotment of Shares
•   Basis of allotment finalized (oversubscription handled
proportionately)
(8) Listing on Stock Exchange
• Shares listed on exchanges like the National Stock Exchange or
Bombay Stock Exchange
• Trading begins in the secondary market
CO-1,2 ;   b   Buyback of Shares (5 Marks)
[10]
BL-2   Meaning (1 Mark)
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Buyback of shares refers to the process by which a company
repurchases   its own shares from existing shareholders at a
predetermined price, as per regulations laid down by the Securities and
Exchange Board of India.
Features (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Can be carried out through tender offer, open market purchase,
or book-building process
• Governed by SEBI (Buyback of Securities) Regulations
• Leads to a reduction in share capital
• Improves earnings per share (EPS) and return ratios
•   Subject to limits on debt-equity ratio and quantum of buyback
Significance (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
•   Signals confidence of management in company prospects
• Helps in price stabilization during market volatility
• Provides an exit opportunity to shareholders
• Acts as a tool for capital restructuring
2. Delisting of Securities (5 Marks)
Delisting (1 Mark)
Delisting refers to the removal of a company’s securities from a
recognized stock exchange, making them no longer available for
trading on that exchange under Securities and Exchange Board of India
regulations.
Features (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Can be voluntary (by company promoters) or compulsory
• Governed by SEBI Regulations
• Requires shareholder approval and fair exit price
• Reverse book-building process often used for price discovery
•   Post-delisting, shares become illiquid
Significance (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Allows companies to go private and reduce compliance burden
• Helps in corporate restructuring and consolidation
•   Protects investor interest through exit opportunities
• Removes non-compliant or financially weak firms from
exchanges
National Housing Bank (NHB) (5 Marks)
a
Role (2 Marks)
The National Housing Bank was established in 1988 as an apex
institution for housing finance. It promotes, regulates, and develops
Q4   housing finance institutions and works under the overall supervision of
CO-1,2;   the Reserve Bank of India.   [10]
BL-2   Functions (2 Marks)
• Regulation & Supervision: Oversees housing finance
companies (now largely transferred to RBI).
• Refinance Support: Provides refinance to banks and HFCs for
housing loans.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
• Promotion of Housing Finance: Encourages new institutions
and affordable housing schemes.
•   Securitization: Develops secondary mortgage markets and
liquidity mechanisms.
Importance (1 Mark)
• Promotes housing sector growth and employment
• Enhances financial inclusion through housing finance
• Ensures stability and development of housing finance system
Association of Merchant Bankers of India (AMBI) (5 Marks)
Role (2 Marks)
The Association of Merchant Bankers of India is a professional body
representing merchant bankers in India. It functions under the
regulatory framework of the Securities and Exchange Board of India
to promote healthy practices in merchant banking.
Functions (2 Marks)
• Code of Conduct: Sets ethical standards for merchant bankers
• Industry Representation: Acts as a bridge between SEBI and
members
• Training &   Development:   Conducts   seminars   and
professional programs
• Regulatory Support: Facilitates compliance with SEBI
guidelines
Importance (1 Mark)
• Ensures efficient functioning of capital markets
• Builds investor confidence through transparency
• Promotes professionalism in merchant banking
Role of the Secondary Market in the Indian Financial System
The secondary market refers to the segment of the capital market where
existing securities are traded among investors, rather than being issued
for the first time. In India, major secondary markets include the
Bombay Stock Exchange and the National Stock Exchange, regulated
by the Securities and Exchange Board of India. It plays a crucial
supportive role in the overall financial system by enhancing efficiency,
investor confidence, and capital formation.
(10 Marks; Any Five; 2 Mark each)
CO-1, 2;   1. Promotion of Liquidity
b   [10]
BL-4   Liquidity refers to the ease with which securities can be bought or sold
without significant price changes.
• Secondary markets provide continuous trading platforms,
enabling investors to convert securities into cash quickly.
• High liquidity reduces investment risk and encourages
participation from both retail and institutional investors.
•   This, in turn, boosts primary market activity, as investors are
more willing to subscribe to new issues knowing they can exit
easily.
2. Ensuring Price Discovery
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
Price discovery is the process of determining the fair market value of
securities through demand and supply forces.
• Secondary markets facilitate real-time price formation based on
information, expectations, and trading activity.
• Transparent electronic trading systems (like NSE’s screen-
based trading) ensure efficient and unbiased pricing.
• This helps in allocating resources efficiently across sectors.
3. Facilitating Transfer of Securities
• Secondary markets enable the transfer of ownership of
securities from one investor to another.
• With the introduction of dematerialization through National
Securities Depository Limited and Central Depository Services
Limited, transfers have become:
o Faster
o Safer
o Paperless
• This reduces transaction costs and operational risks.
4. Strengthening the Capital Market
• A vibrant secondary market enhances investor confidence,
which indirectly supports the primary market.
•   It provides exit options, encouraging long-term investments in
equity.
•   It attracts foreign portfolio investors (FPIs), thereby integrating
the Indian market with global financial systems.
• Improved market depth and breadth lead to overall capital
market development.
5. Importance
While the secondary market is indispensable, some limitations exist:
•   Speculative trading can lead to volatility.
• Market manipulation risks, though controlled by SEBI, still
exist.
a   1. Suitability of DFIs over Commercial Banks (3 Marks)
Development Financial Institutions are better suited for such projects
due to the following reasons:
• Long-term Financing Orientation:
DFIs specialize in medium- and long-term lending, unlike
commercial banks that rely on short-term deposits and face
Q5   asset-liability mismatch issues.
CO-1,2;   • Developmental Mandate:   [10]
BL-2   DFIs are driven by economic and social objectives, not just
profit maximization.
• Higher Risk Appetite:
DFIs are equipped to finance projects with long gestation
periods and uncertain returns, which commercial banks
typically avoid.
2. Financial and Developmental Roles of DFIs (4 Marks)
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
DFIs play a dual role financial and developmental:
A. Financial Roles (2 Marks)
•   Project   Financing:   Provide   long-term   loans,   equity
participation, or hybrid instruments.
• Refinancing Support: Extend refinance to other institutions
involved.
• Syndication of Funds: Mobilize resources by coordinating with
multiple lenders.
B. Developmental Roles (2 Marks)
• Technical Assistance: Conduct feasibility studies, project
appraisal, and monitoring.
• Capacity Building: Support state agencies and local bodies in
project execution.
• Catalytic Role: Encourage private participation and attract
additional investment.
• Balanced Regional Development: Promote infrastructure in
underserved rural areas.
3. Risks in Long-Term Infrastructure Financing & DFI
Management (3 Marks)
Risks (Any Three;1.5 Marks)
•   Project Risk: Delays in construction, cost overruns.
•   Financial Risk: Interest rate fluctuations, funding mismatches.
•   Political/Policy Risk: Changes in government policies or
priorities.
• Demand Risk: Lower-than-expected usage (e.g., rural roads).
Risk Management Strategies (Any Three; 1.5 Marks)
• Robust Appraisal & Monitoring: Detailed techno-economic
feasibility analysis.
•   Structured Financing: Use of phased disbursements and
milestone-based funding.
• Risk Sharing: Co-financing with multilateral agencies or
government guarantees.
• Hedging & ALM Practices: Manage interest rate and maturity
risks.
•   Policy Support: Secure long-term agreements and regulatory
clearances.
The relationship between monetary policy and the money market in
b
India is both instrumental and transmission-oriented. Monetary policy,
formulated by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), operates largely
CO-1, 2;   through the money market, which acts as the immediate arena where
[10]
BL-2   policy signals are implemented and transmitted to the broader financial
system.
(10 Marks; Any Four; 2.5 Mark each)
1. Conceptual Linkage
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
• Monetary policy refers to actions by the RBI to regulate money
supply and credit conditions to achieve objectives like price
stability, growth, and financial stability.
• The money market is the market for short-term funds (maturity
up to 1 year), including instruments such as Treasury Bills,
Commercial Paper, and Call Money.
• The money market serves as the operational field for monetary
policy.
2. Transmission Mechanism
• The RBI uses policy rates (repo rate, reverse repo rate, MSF) to
influence short-term interest rates.
• These rates directly affect the call money market and other
short-term instruments.
• Changes in money market rates are transmitted to:
o Bank lending rates
o Deposit rates
o Investment and consumption decisions
Thus, the money market acts as the first channel of transmission of
monetary policy.
3. Liquidity Management Role
• The RBI manages liquidity through tools like:
o Open Market Operations
o Liquidity Adjustment Facility
• These operations are conducted in the money market to:
o   Inject liquidity
o Absorb liquidity
4. Interest Rate Determination
• Short-term interest rates in the money market are guided by RBI
policy rates.
• A well-developed money market ensures:
o Efficient price discovery
o Alignment of market rates with policy stance
Thus, monetary policy anchors the interest rate structure of the money
market.
a   The Indian Financial System (IFS) refers to the network of institutions,
markets, instruments, and services that facilitate the mobilization of
savings and their efficient allocation into productive investments. Its
structure can be understood through four core constituents:
1. Financial Institutions (3 Marks)
Q6   These are intermediaries that mobilize savings and allocate credit.
CO-1   • Banking Institutions: Commercial banks, cooperative banks,   [10]
BL-3   regional rural banks
• Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs)
• Development Financial Institutions (DFIs): e.g., NABARD,
SIDBI
• Insurance Companies & Mutual Funds
Role in Development:
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
They channel household savings into investment, provide credit to
agriculture, MSMEs, and industries, and support financial inclusion—
thus fostering capital formation and economic growth.
2. Financial Markets (2 Marks)
Markets where financial assets are traded.
• Money Market (short-term funds): Treasury Bills, Commercial
Papers
•   Capital Market (long-term funds): Equity market, debt market
Role in Development:
They ensure efficient price discovery, liquidity, and optimal allocation
of resources, enabling businesses and government to raise funds for
development projects.
3. Financial Instruments / Assets (2 Marks)
These are the claims or securities traded in financial markets.
• Primary Securities: Shares, debentures
• Secondary Securities: Bank deposits, mutual fund units
•   Derivatives: Futures, options
Role in Development:
They provide diverse investment avenues, risk-return choices, and
facilitate risk management, thereby encouraging savings and
investment.
4. Financial Services (1.5 Marks)
Services that support financial transactions and decision-making.
• Merchant banking, leasing, factoring
•   Credit rating, stock broking
• Investment advisory and wealth management
Role in Development:
They enhance efficiency, reduce transaction costs, and improve
investor confidence, leading to better functioning of financial markets.
5. Financial Regulators (1.5 Marks)
Authorities governing and supervising the system.
• RBI (banking and monetary policy)
• SEBI (capital markets)
• IRDAI (insurance)
• PFRDA (pensions)
Role in Development:
They ensure stability, transparency, and investor protection, which are
crucial for sustained economic growth.
Concept of Fee-Based Services (4 marks)
b
Fee-based services refer to financial services provided by banks and
other financial institutions where income is earned through fees,
commissions, or charges, rather than through interest on lending or
investment activities. These services are also called non-fund based
CO-1, 2;   activities, as they do not involve deployment of the institution’s own
[10]
BL-2   funds.
Features:
• Generate non-interest income
• Involve low risk compared to fund-based activities.
• Enhance customer convenience and financial intermediation
efficiency.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
• Help financial institutions reduce dependence on traditional
lending.
Types and Examples of Fee-Based Services
(6 Marks; Any Three; 2 Mark each)
1. Agency Services
Financial institutions act as agents on behalf of customers.
o Collection of cheques, dividends, interest
o Payment of insurance premiums, utility bills
o Example: Banks collecting LIC premiums or handling
ECS mandates
2. General Utility Services
Provide convenience and support services.
o Safe deposit lockers
o Issue of demand drafts, pay orders
o ATM/debit/credit card services
o Example: Annual locker rent or card issuance fees
3. Guarantee and Letter of Credit Services
Banks provide guarantees or LCs for trade and business
transactions.
o Earn commission without lending funds directly
o Example: Bank guarantee issued for a contractor
a   The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) and the Securities and Exchange
Board of India (SEBI) are two apex regulatory institutions in the Indian
financial system. While RBI governs the monetary and banking
system, SEBI regulates the capital markets and the investor protection
framework.
1. Objectives (2 Marks)
• RBI:
o To maintain monetary stability and control inflation.
o To ensure financial stability and smooth functioning of
the banking system.
Q7   • SEBI:
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-3   o To protect investor interests.
o To promote the development and regulation of securities
markets.
2. Functions (3 Marks)
• RBI Functions:
o Issue of currency notes.
o Banker to the Government and banks.
o Controller of credit through monetary policy tools
(CRR, SLR, repo rate).
o Custodian of foreign exchange reserves.
• SEBI Functions:
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---
o Regulates stock exchanges and intermediaries (brokers,
mutual funds).
o Registers and supervises market participants.
o Prevents fraudulent and unfair trade practices.
o Regulates IPOs and capital raising activities.
3. Powers (2 Marks)
• RBI Powers:
o Can regulate and supervise banks.
o Authority to frame monetary policy and issue directives
to banks.
o Can impose penalties and restrict banking operations.
• SEBI Powers:
o Can investigate and penalize market misconduct.
o Power to ban entities from trading in securities markets.
o Authority to regulate listing, trading, and disclosure
requirements.
4. Role in the Indian Financial System (3 Marks)
• RBI Role:
o Acts as the central bank, ensuring liquidity and systemic
stability.
o Maintains confidence in the currency and banking
system.
o Plays a developmental role in financial inclusion.
• SEBI Role:
o Ensures fair, transparent, and efficient capital markets.
o Builds investor confidence through regulation and
disclosure norms.
o   Facilitates capital formation and market development.
Process of Formation of SHGs (6 Marks)
b
The formation of Self-Help Groups (SHGs) is a systematic and
participatory process aimed at promoting financial inclusion and
grassroots empowerment, particularly among weaker sections.
Step-wise Process:
1. Identification of Members
•   Typically consists of 10–20 individuals (preferably from
similar socio-economic backgrounds).
CO-1, 2;   • Members are usually homogeneous (e.g., women, farmers,
[10]
BL-3   labourers).
• Emphasis is on mutual trust and shared objectives.
2. Group Formation and Orientation
•   Facilitated by NGOs, banks, or government agencies (e.g.,
NABARD initiatives).
• Members are educated about:
o Objectives of SHGs
o Benefits of savings and credit discipline
o Group norms and responsibilities
********** 14 **********
--- page 15 ---
3. Framing of Rules and Regulations
• SHG develops its own bylaws, including:
o Meeting frequency (weekly/monthly)
o Savings contribution amount
o Loan terms and interest rates
• Promotes democratic decision-making.
4. Regular Savings Mobilization
• Each member contributes a fixed small amount regularly.
• Funds are pooled to create a common corpus.
5. Internal Lending
• SHG begins lending to members from pooled savings.
• Loans are given for:
o Consumption needs
o Income-generating activities
• Builds credit discipline and financial literacy.
6. Bank Linkage
•   After demonstrating stability (usually 6 months):
o SHG opens a bank account
o Becomes eligible for bank credit (SHG-Bank Linkage
Programme)
• Enhances access to formal financial services.
7. Capacity Building and Grading
• Performance is periodically assessed.
• Training is provided for:
o Bookkeeping
o Financial management
o Entrepreneurship
Types of any two SHGs in details
(4 Marks; Any Two; 2 Mark each)
1. Economic SHGs
2. Self-Managed SHGs
********** 15 **********
````
