````text
Q1   [20]
Definition of Financial Inclusion (1 mark)   [05]
a.
Financial inclusion refers to the process of ensuring access to
appropriate, affordable, and timely financial services such as banking,
credit, insurance, and payment services to all sections of society,
particularly the vulnerable and low-income groups, at a reasonable cost
in a transparent manner.
Initiatives to Enlarge Financial Inclusion in India
(4 Marks; Any Four; 1 mark each)
1. Banking Outreach & No-Frills Accounts
o Simplified accounts with zero or minimum balance
requirements to bring unbanked people into the formal
CO-1,2;   system.
BL-2   2. Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY)
o Massive financial inclusion programme (2014) focusing
on universal banking access, RuPay cards, overdraft
facilities, and direct benefit transfers (DBT).
3. Business Correspondent (BC) Model
o Use of agents to provide banking services in remote and
rural areas, reducing the need for physical bank
branches.
4. Digital Payment Initiatives
o Introduction of UPI, AEPS, mobile banking, and RuPay
cards to promote cashless and accessible financial
transactions.
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
5. Direct Benefit Transfer
o Transfer   of government   subsidies   directly   into
beneficiaries’ bank accounts, improving transparency
and inclusion.
6. Microfinance & Self-Help Groups (SHGs)
o Provision of small loans to low-income individuals,
especially women,   through SHG-Bank   linkage
programmes.
7. Financial Literacy and Credit Counselling
o Efforts by RBI and other institutions to educate people
about financial products and responsible usage.
A firm should prefer recourse factoring over non-recourse factoring   [05]
in situations where cost minimisation and stronger control over credit
risk are important. Under recourse factoring, the firm retains the risk
of bad debts, making it cheaper than non-recourse factoring.
Situations: (Any two; 2.5 marks each)
1. When the firm has strong credit control systems
If the firm is confident about its customers’ creditworthiness
and has efficient receivables management, it can bear the credit
risk and opt for the cheaper recourse option.
2. When   the   incidence   of   bad   debts   is   low
Firms dealing with reliable, long-term customers or operating
CO-1,2 ;   in stable industries face minimal default risk, making recourse
b.
BL-1   factoring more suitable.
3. When   cost   considerations   are   critical
Recourse factoring involves lower factoring charges since the
factor does not assume credit risk, helping firms reduce
financing costs.
4. When the firm wants only financing and collection services
If the firm does not require credit risk protection but only
liquidity and receivables management, recourse factoring is
appropriate.
5. When the firm is willing to retain risk for higher returns
By bearing the risk, firms avoid higher fees and can improve
profitability compared to non-recourse arrangements.
CO-1; BL-   Major Challenges of the Indian Financial System   [05]
1   c.
(5 Marks; Any Five; 1 mark each)
1. High   Level   of   Non-Performing   Assets   (NPAs)
Indian banks, particularly public sector banks, have historically
faced high NPAs, which have weakened profitability, reduced
lending capacity, and affected financial stability.
2. Financial   Exclusion
Despite initiatives like Jan Dhan Yojana, a significant portion
of the population still lacks access to formal financial services,
especially in rural and informal sectors.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
3. Inefficient   Financial   Intermediation
The transmission of monetary policy is often weak due to rigid
interest rates, structural bottlenecks, and dominance of banks
over capital markets.
4. Underdeveloped   Capital   Markets
Compared to advanced economies, India’s corporate bond
market remains shallow, limiting long-term financing options
for infrastructure and industry.
5. Regulatory   and   Structural   Issues
Multiplicity of regulators (RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, etc.) and
coordination challenges can lead to regulatory overlaps and
gaps.
Mortgage (2 marks)   [05]
d.
A mortgage is the transfer of an interest in specific immovable property
(such as land or building) by a borrower (mortgagor) to a lender
(mortgagee) as security for a loan. The ownership is not fully
transferred; only a limited interest is created to secure repayment.
Upon repayment of the loan, the interest is extinguished, and the
property reverts fully to the borrower.
One Mortgage Instrument (3 marks):
A simple mortgage is one of the most common forms of mortgage in
India.
CO-1, 2 ;   Features:
BL-2   1. The mortgagor does not deliver possession of the property to
the lender.
2. The borrower personally binds himself to repay the loan.
3. In case of default, the mortgagee has the right to cause the
property to be sold through legal process.
4. The lender cannot take possession but can approach the court
for recovery.
Example:
A person takes a housing loan from a bank and creates a charge on their
house as security. If he fails to repay, the bank can sell the property
through legal means.
a   Debt Instruments (5 marks)
Meaning:
Debt instruments represent a contractual obligation whereby the issuer
(borrower) promises to pay periodic interest and repay the principal to
the investor (lender).
Q2   Types in India:
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-2   • Government Securities (G-Secs): Issued by Central/State
Governments
• Corporate Bonds/Debentures: Issued by companies
• Money Market Instruments: Treasury Bills, Commercial
Paper (CP), Certificates of Deposit (CD)
Features:
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
• Fixed income (interest-bearing)
• Defined maturity
• Lower risk (especially G-Secs)
Role in the Indian Financial System:
• Mobilization of savings: Channels household and institutional
savings into productive uses
• Financing fiscal deficit: Government relies on G-Secs
• Benchmark for interest rates: G-Sec yields act as a reference
for pricing other instruments
• Liquidity management: Used by RBI in Open Market
Operations (OMO)
Derivatives (5 marks)
Meaning:
Derivatives are financial contracts whose value is derived from an
underlying asset such as equity, interest rates, currency, or
commodities.
Types in India:
•   Futures and Options (Equity & Index derivatives)
• Currency derivatives
•   Interest Rate Futures (IRFs)
Features:
• No initial full investment (margin-based trading)
• Used for hedging, speculation, and arbitrage
• High leverage
Role in the Indian Financial System:
• Risk management: Helps firms hedge against price, interest
rate, and exchange rate risks
• Price discovery: Reflects market expectations about future
prices
• Market efficiency: Enhances liquidity and depth of financial
markets
• Integration with global markets: Aligns Indian markets with
international practices
b   1. Venture Capital (1 Mark)
Venture Capital (VC) refers to risk capital provided to high-growth,
innovative Startups and small businesses with long-term potential. It is
typically equity-based financing, where investors participate in both
risk and returns.
2. Role of Venture Capital in the Indian Financial System
CO-1,2;
(3 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each) [10]
BL-3
Venture capital plays a pivotal role in strengthening India’s financial
architecture:
• Bridging Financing Gap: Provides funds to startups lacking
access to traditional sources like banks or capital markets.
• Promoting Innovation: Encourages technology-driven and
knowledge-based industries (e.g., fintech, biotech).
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
• Employment Generation: Supports startups that create jobs
and boost economic development.
• Capital Market Development: Successful VC-backed firms
often go for IPOs, deepening capital markets.
• Risk Diversification: Channels funds into high-risk, high-
return ventures, improving allocation efficiency.
3. Importance for Entrepreneurial Growth
(3 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each)
Venture capital is crucial for entrepreneurship due to the following
reasons:
• Access to Risk Capital: Entrepreneurs often lack collateral;
VC provides funding without requiring security.
• Managerial & Technical Support: VC firms offer mentorship,
governance, and strategic guidance.
• Market Credibility: Association with reputed VC firms
enhances trust among stakeholders.
• Long-Term Orientation: Unlike banks, VC investors are
patient and focus on growth rather than immediate returns.
• Encouragement of Innovation Ecosystem: Supports Startups
in emerging sectors, fostering a culture of innovation in India.
4. Stages of Venture Capital Financing (3 Marks)
Venture capital financing typically proceeds through the following
stages:
(i) Seed Stage
•   Initial funding for idea development, research, and prototype
creation.
• High risk, as the business model is not yet proven.
(ii) Start-up Stage
• Financing for product development and initial marketing.
• The firm begins operations but may not generate profits.
(iii) Early Growth Stage (First Stage Financing)
• Funds used for scaling production and expanding market reach.
• Company starts generating revenue.
(iv) Expansion Stage (Second/Third Stage Financing)
•   Capital for expansion, diversification, and increasing capacity.
• Risk reduces as the business stabilizes.
(v) Exit Stage
• VC investors exit through IPO, mergers, or acquisitions.
•   Realization of returns on investment.
An Initial Public Offer (IPO) is the first sale of securities by a company
a   to the public in the primary market. The investment banker (also called
merchant banker) plays a central role in managing, structuring, and
Q3
CO-1, 2;   executing the issue.   [10]
BL-3   1. Role of Investment Banker in IPO
(4 Marks; Any Three; 1 Mark each)
As an investment banker, I would assist the company in the following
ways:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
(a) Issue Planning & Structuring
• Assess capital requirements and decide issue size, pricing, and
timing
• Choose between fixed price issue or book-building method
(b) Due Diligence
• Conduct financial, legal, and business verification
• Ensure compliance with Securities and Exchange Board of
India regulations
(c) Drafting Offer Document
• Prepare Draft Red Herring Prospectus (DRHP)
•   Disclose all material information for investor protection
(d) Underwriting & Syndication
• Arrange underwriters to guarantee subscription
• Appoint intermediaries: brokers, registrars, bankers
(e) Marketing the Issue
• Conduct roadshows, investor meetings, and advertisements
• Build investor confidence and demand
2. Steps in Public Issue Process (6 Marks)
The IPO process involves the following sequential steps:
(1) Appointment of Intermediaries
• Merchant banker, registrar, underwriters, legal advisors are
appointed
(2) Preparation & Filing of DRHP
•   Draft prospectus submitted to SEBI for approval
• SEBI reviews disclosures and compliance
(3) SEBI Approval
• Observations given by SEBI
• Company incorporates required changes
(4) Pricing of Issue
•   Final price determined:
o Fixed price OR
o Book building process (price discovery through bids)
(5) Marketing & Roadshows
• Promotion through institutional and retail investor outreach
(6) Opening of IPO
•   Public subscription opens for a fixed period (usually 3–5 days)
(7) Allotment of Shares
•   Basis of allotment finalized (oversubscription handled
proportionately)
(8) Listing on Stock Exchange
• Shares listed on exchanges like the National Stock Exchange or
Bombay Stock Exchange
• Trading begins in the secondary market
CO-1,2 ;   b   Buyback of Shares (5 Marks)
[10]
BL-2   Meaning (1 Mark)
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Buyback of shares refers to the process by which a company
repurchases   its own shares from existing shareholders at a
predetermined price, as per regulations laid down by the Securities and
Exchange Board of India.
Features (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Can be carried out through tender offer, open market purchase,
or book-building process
• Governed by SEBI (Buyback of Securities) Regulations
• Leads to a reduction in share capital
• Improves earnings per share (EPS) and return ratios
•   Subject to limits on debt-equity ratio and quantum of buyback
Significance (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
•   Signals confidence of management in company prospects
• Helps in price stabilization during market volatility
• Provides an exit opportunity to shareholders
• Acts as a tool for capital restructuring
2. Delisting of Securities (5 Marks)
Delisting (1 Mark)
Delisting refers to the removal of a company’s securities from a
recognized stock exchange, making them no longer available for
trading on that exchange under Securities and Exchange Board of India
regulations.
Features (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Can be voluntary (by company promoters) or compulsory
• Governed by SEBI Regulations
• Requires shareholder approval and fair exit price
• Reverse book-building process often used for price discovery
•   Post-delisting, shares become illiquid
Significance (2 Marks; Any Two; 1 Mark each)
• Allows companies to go private and reduce compliance burden
• Helps in corporate restructuring and consolidation
•   Protects investor interest through exit opportunities
• Removes non-compliant or financially weak firms from
exchanges
National Housing Bank (NHB) (5 Marks)
a
Role (2 Marks)
The National Housing Bank was established in 1988 as an apex
institution for housing finance. It promotes, regulates, and develops
Q4   housing finance institutions and works under the overall supervision of
CO-1,2;   the Reserve Bank of India.   [10]
BL-2   Functions (2 Marks)
• Regulation & Supervision: Oversees housing finance
companies (now largely transferred to RBI).
• Refinance Support: Provides refinance to banks and HFCs for
housing loans.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
• Promotion of Housing Finance: Encourages new institutions
and affordable housing schemes.
•   Securitization: Develops secondary mortgage markets and
liquidity mechanisms.
Importance (1 Mark)
• Promotes housing sector growth and employment
• Enhances financial inclusion through housing finance
• Ensures stability and development of housing finance system
Association of Merchant Bankers of India (AMBI) (5 Marks)
Role (2 Marks)
The Association of Merchant Bankers of India is a professional body
representing merchant bankers in India. It functions under the
regulatory framework of the Securities and Exchange Board of India
to promote healthy practices in merchant banking.
Functions (2 Marks)
• Code of Conduct: Sets ethical standards for merchant bankers
• Industry Representation: Acts as a bridge between SEBI and
members
• Training &   Development:   Conducts   seminars   and
professional programs
• Regulatory Support: Facilitates compliance with SEBI
guidelines
Importance (1 Mark)
• Ensures efficient functioning of capital markets
• Builds investor confidence through transparency
• Promotes professionalism in merchant banking
Role of the Secondary Market in the Indian Financial System
The secondary market refers to the segment of the capital market where
existing securities are traded among investors, rather than being issued
for the first time. In India, major secondary markets include the
Bombay Stock Exchange and the National Stock Exchange, regulated
by the Securities and Exchange Board of India. It plays a crucial
supportive role in the overall financial system by enhancing efficiency,
investor confidence, and capital formation.
(10 Marks; Any Five; 2 Mark each)
CO-1, 2;   1. Promotion of Liquidity
b   [10]
BL-4   Liquidity refers to the ease with which securities can be bought or sold
without significant price changes.
• Secondary markets provide continuous trading platforms,
enabling investors to convert securities into cash quickly.
• High liquidity reduces investment risk and encourages
participation from both retail and institutional investors.
•   This, in turn, boosts primary market activity, as investors are
more willing to subscribe to new issues knowing they can exit
easily.
2. Ensuring Price Discovery
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
Price discovery is the process of determining the fair market value of
securities through demand and supply forces.
• Secondary markets facilitate real-time price formation based on
information, expectations, and trading activity.
• Transparent electronic trading systems (like NSE’s screen-
based trading) ensure efficient and unbiased pricing.
• This helps in allocating resources efficiently across sectors.
3. Facilitating Transfer of Securities
• Secondary markets enable the transfer of ownership of
securities from one investor to another.
• With the introduction of dematerialization through National
Securities Depository Limited and Central Depository Services
Limited, transfers have become:
o Faster
o Safer
o Paperless
• This reduces transaction costs and operational risks.
4. Strengthening the Capital Market
• A vibrant secondary market enhances investor confidence,
which indirectly supports the primary market.
•   It provides exit options, encouraging long-term investments in
equity.
•   It attracts foreign portfolio investors (FPIs), thereby integrating
the Indian market with global financial systems.
• Improved market depth and breadth lead to overall capital
market development.
5. Importance
While the secondary market is indispensable, some limitations exist:
•   Speculative trading can lead to volatility.
• Market manipulation risks, though controlled by SEBI, still
exist.
a   1. Suitability of DFIs over Commercial Banks (3 Marks)
Development Financial Institutions are better suited for such projects
due to the following reasons:
• Long-term Financing Orientation:
DFIs specialize in medium- and long-term lending, unlike
commercial banks that rely on short-term deposits and face
Q5   asset-liability mismatch issues.
CO-1,2;   • Developmental Mandate:   [10]
BL-2   DFIs are driven by economic and social objectives, not just
profit maximization.
• Higher Risk Appetite:
DFIs are equipped to finance projects with long gestation
periods and uncertain returns, which commercial banks
typically avoid.
2. Financial and Developmental Roles of DFIs (4 Marks)
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
DFIs play a dual role financial and developmental:
A. Financial Roles (2 Marks)
•   Project   Financing:   Provide   long-term   loans,   equity
participation, or hybrid instruments.
• Refinancing Support: Extend refinance to other institutions
involved.
• Syndication of Funds: Mobilize resources by coordinating with
multiple lenders.
B. Developmental Roles (2 Marks)
• Technical Assistance: Conduct feasibility studies, project
appraisal, and monitoring.
• Capacity Building: Support state agencies and local bodies in
project execution.
• Catalytic Role: Encourage private participation and attract
additional investment.
• Balanced Regional Development: Promote infrastructure in
underserved rural areas.
3. Risks in Long-Term Infrastructure Financing & DFI
Management (3 Marks)
Risks (Any Three;1.5 Marks)
•   Project Risk: Delays in construction, cost overruns.
•   Financial Risk: Interest rate fluctuations, funding mismatches.
•   Political/Policy Risk: Changes in government policies or
priorities.
• Demand Risk: Lower-than-expected usage (e.g., rural roads).
Risk Management Strategies (Any Three; 1.5 Marks)
• Robust Appraisal & Monitoring: Detailed techno-economic
feasibility analysis.
•   Structured Financing: Use of phased disbursements and
milestone-based funding.
• Risk Sharing: Co-financing with multilateral agencies or
government guarantees.
• Hedging & ALM Practices: Manage interest rate and maturity
risks.
•   Policy Support: Secure long-term agreements and regulatory
clearances.
The relationship between monetary policy and the money market in
b
India is both instrumental and transmission-oriented. Monetary policy,
formulated by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), operates largely
CO-1, 2;   through the money market, which acts as the immediate arena where
[10]
BL-2   policy signals are implemented and transmitted to the broader financial
system.
(10 Marks; Any Four; 2.5 Mark each)
1. Conceptual Linkage
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
• Monetary policy refers to actions by the RBI to regulate money
supply and credit conditions to achieve objectives like price
stability, growth, and financial stability.
• The money market is the market for short-term funds (maturity
up to 1 year), including instruments such as Treasury Bills,
Commercial Paper, and Call Money.
• The money market serves as the operational field for monetary
policy.
2. Transmission Mechanism
• The RBI uses policy rates (repo rate, reverse repo rate, MSF) to
influence short-term interest rates.
• These rates directly affect the call money market and other
short-term instruments.
• Changes in money market rates are transmitted to:
o Bank lending rates
o Deposit rates
o Investment and consumption decisions
Thus, the money market acts as the first channel of transmission of
monetary policy.
3. Liquidity Management Role
• The RBI manages liquidity through tools like:
o Open Market Operations
o Liquidity Adjustment Facility
• These operations are conducted in the money market to:
o   Inject liquidity
o Absorb liquidity
4. Interest Rate Determination
• Short-term interest rates in the money market are guided by RBI
policy rates.
• A well-developed money market ensures:
o Efficient price discovery
o Alignment of market rates with policy stance
Thus, monetary policy anchors the interest rate structure of the money
market.
a   The Indian Financial System (IFS) refers to the network of institutions,
markets, instruments, and services that facilitate the mobilization of
savings and their efficient allocation into productive investments. Its
structure can be understood through four core constituents:
1. Financial Institutions (3 Marks)
Q6   These are intermediaries that mobilize savings and allocate credit.
CO-1   • Banking Institutions: Commercial banks, cooperative banks,   [10]
BL-3   regional rural banks
• Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs)
• Development Financial Institutions (DFIs): e.g., NABARD,
SIDBI
• Insurance Companies & Mutual Funds
Role in Development:
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
They channel household savings into investment, provide credit to
agriculture, MSMEs, and industries, and support financial inclusion—
thus fostering capital formation and economic growth.
2. Financial Markets (2 Marks)
Markets where financial assets are traded.
• Money Market (short-term funds): Treasury Bills, Commercial
Papers
•   Capital Market (long-term funds): Equity market, debt market
Role in Development:
They ensure efficient price discovery, liquidity, and optimal allocation
of resources, enabling businesses and government to raise funds for
development projects.
3. Financial Instruments / Assets (2 Marks)
These are the claims or securities traded in financial markets.
• Primary Securities: Shares, debentures
• Secondary Securities: Bank deposits, mutual fund units
•   Derivatives: Futures, options
Role in Development:
They provide diverse investment avenues, risk-return choices, and
facilitate risk management, thereby encouraging savings and
investment.
4. Financial Services (1.5 Marks)
Services that support financial transactions and decision-making.
• Merchant banking, leasing, factoring
•   Credit rating, stock broking
• Investment advisory and wealth management
Role in Development:
They enhance efficiency, reduce transaction costs, and improve
investor confidence, leading to better functioning of financial markets.
5. Financial Regulators (1.5 Marks)
Authorities governing and supervising the system.
• RBI (banking and monetary policy)
• SEBI (capital markets)
• IRDAI (insurance)
• PFRDA (pensions)
Role in Development:
They ensure stability, transparency, and investor protection, which are
crucial for sustained economic growth.
Concept of Fee-Based Services (4 marks)
b
Fee-based services refer to financial services provided by banks and
other financial institutions where income is earned through fees,
commissions, or charges, rather than through interest on lending or
investment activities. These services are also called non-fund based
CO-1, 2;   activities, as they do not involve deployment of the institution’s own
[10]
BL-2   funds.
Features:
• Generate non-interest income
• Involve low risk compared to fund-based activities.
• Enhance customer convenience and financial intermediation
efficiency.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
• Help financial institutions reduce dependence on traditional
lending.
Types and Examples of Fee-Based Services
(6 Marks; Any Three; 2 Mark each)
1. Agency Services
Financial institutions act as agents on behalf of customers.
o Collection of cheques, dividends, interest
o Payment of insurance premiums, utility bills
o Example: Banks collecting LIC premiums or handling
ECS mandates
2. General Utility Services
Provide convenience and support services.
o Safe deposit lockers
o Issue of demand drafts, pay orders
o ATM/debit/credit card services
o Example: Annual locker rent or card issuance fees
3. Guarantee and Letter of Credit Services
Banks provide guarantees or LCs for trade and business
transactions.
o Earn commission without lending funds directly
o Example: Bank guarantee issued for a contractor
a   The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) and the Securities and Exchange
Board of India (SEBI) are two apex regulatory institutions in the Indian
financial system. While RBI governs the monetary and banking
system, SEBI regulates the capital markets and the investor protection
framework.
1. Objectives (2 Marks)
• RBI:
o To maintain monetary stability and control inflation.
o To ensure financial stability and smooth functioning of
the banking system.
Q7   • SEBI:
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-3   o To protect investor interests.
o To promote the development and regulation of securities
markets.
2. Functions (3 Marks)
• RBI Functions:
o Issue of currency notes.
o Banker to the Government and banks.
o Controller of credit through monetary policy tools
(CRR, SLR, repo rate).
o Custodian of foreign exchange reserves.
• SEBI Functions:
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---
o Regulates stock exchanges and intermediaries (brokers,
mutual funds).
o Registers and supervises market participants.
o Prevents fraudulent and unfair trade practices.
o Regulates IPOs and capital raising activities.
3. Powers (2 Marks)
• RBI Powers:
o Can regulate and supervise banks.
o Authority to frame monetary policy and issue directives
to banks.
o Can impose penalties and restrict banking operations.
• SEBI Powers:
o Can investigate and penalize market misconduct.
o Power to ban entities from trading in securities markets.
o Authority to regulate listing, trading, and disclosure
requirements.
4. Role in the Indian Financial System (3 Marks)
• RBI Role:
o Acts as the central bank, ensuring liquidity and systemic
stability.
o Maintains confidence in the currency and banking
system.
o Plays a developmental role in financial inclusion.
• SEBI Role:
o Ensures fair, transparent, and efficient capital markets.
o Builds investor confidence through regulation and
disclosure norms.
o   Facilitates capital formation and market development.
Process of Formation of SHGs (6 Marks)
b
The formation of Self-Help Groups (SHGs) is a systematic and
participatory process aimed at promoting financial inclusion and
grassroots empowerment, particularly among weaker sections.
Step-wise Process:
1. Identification of Members
•   Typically consists of 10–20 individuals (preferably from
similar socio-economic backgrounds).
CO-1, 2;   • Members are usually homogeneous (e.g., women, farmers,
[10]
BL-3   labourers).
• Emphasis is on mutual trust and shared objectives.
2. Group Formation and Orientation
•   Facilitated by NGOs, banks, or government agencies (e.g.,
NABARD initiatives).
• Members are educated about:
o Objectives of SHGs
o Benefits of savings and credit discipline
o Group norms and responsibilities
********** 14 **********
--- page 15 ---
3. Framing of Rules and Regulations
• SHG develops its own bylaws, including:
o Meeting frequency (weekly/monthly)
o Savings contribution amount
o Loan terms and interest rates
• Promotes democratic decision-making.
4. Regular Savings Mobilization
• Each member contributes a fixed small amount regularly.
• Funds are pooled to create a common corpus.
5. Internal Lending
• SHG begins lending to members from pooled savings.
• Loans are given for:
o Consumption needs
o Income-generating activities
• Builds credit discipline and financial literacy.
6. Bank Linkage
•   After demonstrating stability (usually 6 months):
o SHG opens a bank account
o Becomes eligible for bank credit (SHG-Bank Linkage
Programme)
• Enhances access to formal financial services.
7. Capacity Building and Grading
• Performance is periodically assessed.
• Training is provided for:
o Bookkeeping
o Financial management
o Entrepreneurship
Types of any two SHGs in details
(4 Marks; Any Two; 2 Mark each)
1. Economic SHGs
2. Self-Managed SHGs
````

````text
1. Introduction and Concept (2 Marks)
Payment Banks are a new category of banks introduced by the Reserve Bank
of India (RBI) in 2014.
They are designed to enhance financial inclusion by providing small savings
accounts and payments/remittance services to low-income households,
migrant labourers, and small businesses.
Unlike traditional banks, Payment Banks cannot lend money or issue credit
cards. They operate mainly through digital and technology-driven platforms.
Functions (2 Marks)
• Deposit limit: Can accept deposits up to ₹2 lakh per customer (limit
revised by RBI from time to time).
CO-1,2;   •   Services offered:
a.   [05]
BL-2   o Small savings accounts
o Domestic remittances
o Mobile and online payments
•   Investments: Must invest a major portion of deposits in safe
government securities.
Examples: Airtel Payments Bank, Paytm Payments Bank, etc.
Role in Financial Inclusion (anyone) (1 mark)
• Access to banking: Provides basic banking services in rural and remote
areas through mobile technology and agent networks.
• Low-cost transactions: Reduce dependence on cash and facilitate
digital payments for unbanked populations.
• Remittance services: Help migrant workers send money easily and
safely.
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
• Women empowerment & inclusion: Simplifies account opening and
encourages first-time users into the formal financial system.

CO-1;   1. Introduction – Definition of Financial System (1 mark)
BL-2   A financial system refers to the complex network of financial institutions,
markets, instruments, and services that facilitate the flow of funds between
savers and investors in an economy.
2. Explanation of “Well-Integrated System” (1 mark)
It is called a well-integrated system because all its components — financial
institutions, markets, instruments, and services — are interlinked and
function cohesively to promote economic efficiency and stability.3.
Interdependence of Components (3 marks)
•   Financial institutions (like banks, NBFCs, mutual funds) mobilize
c.   [05]
savings and provide credit.
•   Financial markets (money market, capital market, forex market)
provide platforms for trading financial assets.
•   Financial instruments (shares, bonds, derivatives, etc.) act as vehicles
for investment and liquidity.
•   Financial services (insurance, leasing, factoring, credit rating, etc.)
support the smooth functioning of markets and institutions.
→ Each part relies on the other — for instance, institutions use
markets to raise funds, and markets depend on instruments and
services to operate efficiently.
Fee-based services refer to financial services where institutions earn income
through fees and commissions rather than interest. Examples include
merchant banking, portfolio management, underwriting, leasing, credit rating,
etc.
(A) Advantages – (2.5 Marks)
1. Diversification of Income
CO-1, 2 ;   Financial institutions can earn non-fund-based income through
d.   [05]
BL-1   advisory and consultancy services, reducing dependence on
traditional interest-based sources.
2. Low Risk and High Profit Potential
Since these services do not involve large fund deployment, the risk of
default is minimal, while the returns through fees and commissions
can be substantial.
(B) Disadvantages – (2.5 Marks)
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
1. Revenue Uncertainty
Income depends on market conditions and client demand; hence, fee-
based earnings can be unstable and cyclical.
2. Requirement of Specialized Expertise
These services demand highly skilled professionals and technological
infrastructure, increasing operational costs and limiting participation
to well-equipped institutions.
I. Importance of the Debt Market (6 Marks)
The debt market, also known as the bond market, is a crucial component of
the capital market because it facilitates borrowing and lending of long-term
funds through the issue and trading of debt instruments (such as government
securities, corporate bonds, debentures, and PSU bonds).
1. Source of Long-term Funds
It provides a mechanism for governments, public sector undertakings (PSUs),
and corporates to raise funds for infrastructure and development projects
without diluting ownership, unlike equity.
2. Risk Diversification for Investors
Investors—especially institutional investors—use the debt market to diversify
portfolios, ensuring steady income and lower risk compared to equities.
3. Benchmark for Interest Rates
The government securities (G-Secs) segment of the debt market acts as a
benchmark for yield curves, influencing interest rates across the financial
system.
4. Enhances Monetary Policy Transmission
Debt market movements reflect monetary policy changes, improving liquidity
Q2   management and price discovery in the economy.
CO-1,2;   a   5. Development of Financial System   [10]
BL-2   A well-functioning debt market enhances capital formation, promotes
financial stability, and deepens the capital market structure, complementing
the equity market.
II. Participants in the Debt Market (4 Marks)
The Indian debt market comprises a wide range of issuers and investors:
1. Issuers (2 marks)
• Central Government – Issues Treasury Bills and Government of India
securities.
• State Governments – Issue State Development Loans (SDLs).
• Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs) – Issue bonds for infrastructure
financing.
• Corporates – Issue debentures and bonds for expansion and working
capital.
2. Investors (2 marks)
•   Institutional Investors – Banks, Insurance Companies, Mutual Funds,
Provident Funds.
• Foreign Institutional Investors (FIIs) – Subject to RBI limits.
•   Retail Investors – Increasingly participating through gilt funds and
online platforms.
Financial Lease and Hire Purchase (5 marks)
1. Obligations, Rights, and Responsibilities in a Financial Lease
A financial lease is a long-term lease in which the lessor (owner of the asset)
CO-1,2;
b   allows the lessee to use an asset in exchange for lease payments, while the [10]
BL-4   lessee assumes most of the risks and rewards of ownership.
A. Lessor (Owner of Asset)
• Obligations:
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
1. Purchase or provide the asset to the lessee.
2. Ensure the asset is in usable condition.
3. Maintain legal ownership until lease completion.
• Rights:
1. Receive regular lease payments as per the agreement.
2. Repossess the asset in case of default.
•   Responsibilities:
1. Financing the asset purchase.
2. Structuring lease terms and documenting agreements.
B. Lessee (User of Asset)
• Obligations:
1. Pay agreed lease installments on time.
2. Use the asset as per the terms of the lease.
• Rights:
1. Right to use and operate the asset during the lease period.
2. Option to purchase the asset at the end of the lease (if included
in the contract).
•   Responsibilities:
1. Bear maintenance and operational costs (usually).
2. Assume risk of obsolescence and asset deterioration.

a. Certificate of Deposit (CD) – 5 Marks
Definition:
A Certificate of Deposit (CD) is a short-term, negotiable money market
instrument issued by scheduled commercial banks and select financial
Q3
institutions to raise funds from the public. It is similar to a fixed deposit but
CO-1, 2;   a   [10]
tradable in the secondary market.
BL-1
Features:
1. Issuer: Scheduled commercial banks and select financial institutions.
2. Tenure: Typically 7 days to 1 year.
3. Denomination: Minimum of ₹1 lakh.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
4. Interest Rate: Usually market-determined, higher than savings
deposits.
5. Liquidity: Negotiable and transferable, can be traded before maturity.
Purpose:
• Provides banks with short-term funds.
•   Offers investors a safe and liquid investment option in the money
market.
b. Treasury Bills (T-Bills) – 5 Marks
Definition:
Treasury Bills (T-Bills) are short-term government securities issued by the
Reserve Bank of India on behalf of the Government of India to meet short-
term borrowing requirements. They are discounted instruments, meaning they
are sold at a price lower than face value and redeemed at par.
Key Features:
1. Issuer: Government of India through RBI.
2. Tenure: 91 days, 182 days, and 364 days.
3. Denomination: Minimum ₹25,000.
4. Return: Earned through discounting, not interest payments.
5. Liquidity: Highly liquid and considered risk-free.
Purpose:
• Used by the government to manage short-term cash needs.
• Provides investors a safe and liquid investment avenue.
Concept of Private Placement (2 Marks)
Private placement refers to the issue of securities (shares, debentures, or
bonds) by a company to a select group of investors such as financial
institutions, banks, mutual funds, insurance companies, or high-net-worth
individuals (HNIs), instead of inviting the general public through a public
issue.
It is governed by Section 42 of the Companies Act, 2013 and relevant SEBI
(Issue   of   Capital   and   Disclosure   Requirements)   Regulations.
This method enables firms to raise capital quickly and with lower compliance
costs.
2. Concept of Private Equity (2 Marks)
Private equity refers to investment made by private equity funds or investors
in unlisted companies to acquire ownership and actively participate in
management to improve value before exiting through listing or sale.
CO-1,2 ;   Unlike private placement, which is primarily a fund-raising mechanism,
b   [10]
BL-2   private equity is an ownership-based long-term investment strategy.
3. Features of Private Placement (3 Marks)
1. Limited Investors: Offered to a maximum of 200 investors in a
financial year (excluding qualified institutional buyers).
2. No Public Advertisement: Securities are offered privately without
public marketing or prospectus.
3. Faster and Cost-Effective: Lower issuance costs and reduced
regulatory formalities compared to a public issue.
4. Institutional Participation: Typically subscribed by financial
institutions, banks, mutual funds, and corporates.
5. Regulatory Oversight: Regulated by SEBI and the Companies Act
to ensure transparency and investor protection.
4. Objectives of Private Placement (3 Marks)
1. Quick Mobilization of Funds: Enables companies to raise funds
swiftly for expansion or working capital.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
2. Reduced Issue Cost: Avoids underwriting, advertising, and listing
expenses.
3. Flexibility in Terms: Negotiation possible on interest rate, maturity,
or convertibility with investors.
4. Maintaining Control: Avoids dilution of control associated with
public issues.
1. Introduction (2 Marks)
Self Help Groups (SHGs) are small, voluntary associations—usually of 10–20
members, primarily women—from similar socio-economic backgrounds who
come together to save regularly, lend internally, and access formal credit
collectively.
They form a crucial part of India’s microfinance and financial inclusion
strategy, particularly under programmes like NABARD’s SHG–Bank Linkage
Programme (SBLP) initiated in the early 1990s.

Role in Financial Empowerment (2 Marks)
• Improved Access to Credit: Overcomes barriers of collateral and credit
history.
• Promotion of Savings Habit: Encourages   thrift and resource
management.
•   Micro-Enterprise Development: Enables small business and income-
generating activities.
•   Financial Literacy: Members gain basic financial management and
decision-making skills.
4. Role in Social Empowerment (2 Marks)
•   Collective Decision-Making: Increases confidence and participation in
local governance.
• Gender Equality: Strengthens women’s role within families and
communities.
•   Social Awareness: Promotes education, health, sanitation, and social
justice issues.
• Networking and Solidarity: Builds community-level support systems
for mutual growth.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Factors responsible for the rapid growth of the derivatives market in
India (5 marks)
1. Risk Management Needs
o Derivatives help businesses, investors, and financial institutions
hedge against price, interest rate, and currency fluctuations.
2. Financial Innovation and Globalisation
o Integration with global markets and introduction of new
financial instruments increased demand for derivatives.
3. Regulatory Support
o SEBI’s regulations and organized exchanges like NSE and BSE
provided transparency, standardization, and investor protection.
4. Technological Advancements
o Online trading platforms, electronic settlement, and real-time
data facilitated easy access to derivatives trading.
5. Market Liquidity and Investor Awareness
o Growing participation by institutional and retail investors,
along with awareness of risk management benefits, boosted
market volumes.

1. Objectives and Significance of Development Financial Institutions
(DFIs) in India (4 Marks)
Development Financial   Institutions (DFIs) are specialized financial
institutions established primarily to provide long-term finance for industrial
[10]
Q5   and infrastructural development. They supplement commercial banks by
financing projects that are high-risk or long-term in nature.
CO-1,2;   a
Objectives of DFIs:
BL-2,4   • Provision of Long-Term Finance: DFIs provide capital for industrial
projects requiring long gestation periods.
• Promotion of Priority Sectors: They finance sectors like agriculture,
SMEs, infrastructure, and export-oriented industries that are often
neglected by commercial banks.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
•   Industrial   Growth   and   Modernization:   DFIs   facilitate
modernization, technology upgradation, and diversification of
industries.
• Regional Development: They promote development in backward
regions by financing regional industries.
•   Stability and Confidence in Financial Markets: By acting as
intermediaries between savings and investment, DFIs help stabilize the
financial system.
Significance of DFIs:
• DFIs fill the long-term finance gap that commercial banks cannot
address.
• They help in employment generation through industrial development.
• Encourage balanced regional economic growth.
•   Facilitate the flow of credit to priority sectors, thus supporting
government development programs.
2. Role of NABARD in Promoting Rural Credit and Agricultural
Development (2 Marks)
The National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD)
was established in 1982 under NABARD Act to promote sustainable rural
development through credit and financial support.
Role of NABARD:
• Acts as a refinance institution for banks and cooperatives in rural
areas.
• Develops and strengthens the rural financial infrastructure.
• Monitors the flow of credit to agriculture and allied sectors.
• Supports policy formulation for agricultural credit and rural
development.
3. Functions of NABARD (4 Marks)
1. Refinance Operations: Provides refinance facilities to commercial
banks, RRBs, and cooperative banks for agricultural and rural
development.
2. Supervision of Cooperative Banks: NABARD regulates and
supervises the rural cooperative credit system.
3. Developmental Role: Offers technical and financial support to
improve rural credit delivery mechanisms.
4. Promoting Microfinance: Facilitates Self-Help Groups (SHGs) and
joint liability groups (JLGs) to enhance financial inclusion.
5. Disaster Relief & Crop Insurance: Provides refinance for crop
insurance schemes and disaster relief in rural areas.
Meaning and Concept of Forfaiting (4 marks)
•   Definition:
Forfaiting is a mechanism of trade finance used in international trade
where an exporter sells its medium to long-term receivables (due from
an importer) to a forfaiter (usually a financial institution or bank) at a
discount, on a without-recourse basis. This means the exporter
CO-1, 2;   transfers all risks of non-payment to the forfaiter and receives
b   [10]
BL-2   immediate cash payment.
• Features:
o Used for export receivables (usually 180 days to 5 years).
o Operates on a without recourse basis — exporter is free from
credit risk.
o The receivables are typically guaranteed by the importer’s bank
through an aval or bank guarantee.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
o Instruments used: Bills of exchange or promissory notes.
o Commonly used for capital goods exports, project exports, or
machinery sales.
Example: (1 marks)
An Indian exporter sells machinery to a buyer in Germany on a 3-year credit.
Instead of waiting for periodic payments, the exporter sells the bills to a
forfaiter (say, EXIM Bank of India) at a discount and receives immediate
funds.
2. Advantages of Forfaiting (5 marks)
Advantages   Explanation
1. Eliminates
Exporter transfers risk of default to the forfaiter.
credit risk
2. Immediate   Exporter receives cash up front, improving working
liquidity   capital and cash flow.
3. Simplified   Receivables are removed from the exporter’s balance
balance sheet   sheet, improving financial ratios.
4. No exchange   Forfaiting is usually done in hard currencies (USD,
rate risk   EUR), fixing the exchange rate at the time of sale.
5. Encourages   Provides exporters with confidence to extend credit
exports   terms to foreign buyers without risk exposure.
1. Introduction (2 marks)
The financial system comprises financial institutions, markets, instruments,
and services that facilitate the flow of funds between savers and investors.
It plays a crucial role in mobilizing savings, allocating capital efficiently, and
fostering economic development by ensuring productive investment and
stability in the economy.
2. Contribution of Financial System to Economic Growth (4 marks)
1. Mobilization of Savings:
o Encourages individuals and institutions to save through various
financial instruments.
o Converts idle savings into productive investments.
2. Efficient Allocation of Resources:
o Directs funds toward sectors and enterprises with higher
Q6   returns, ensuring optimal utilization of capital.
3. Facilitation of Investment and Capital Formation:
CO-1   a   [10]
o Provides credit and equity finance for business expansion,
BL-2   technological innovation, and infrastructure development.
4. Encouragement of Entrepreneurship:
o Financial institutions supply risk capital and advisory services
to entrepreneurs.
5. Promotion of Financial Inclusion:
o Increases access to formal finance for rural and underbanked
populations, thus stimulating overall economic participation.
6. Stabilization Function:
o Helps maintain monetary stability through regulation of
liquidity and interest rates by financial intermediaries and the
central bank.
3. Interrelationship between Financial System Development and
Economic Growth (3 marks)
• Two-way relationship:
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
o A developed financial system supports economic growth by
channeling funds efficiently and providing risk management
mechanisms.
o Conversely, economic growth increases demand for financial
services, promoting financial innovation and market deepening.
• Empirical Evidence:
o Studies and historical experience show that countries with more
advanced financial systems, developed banking networks,
vibrant capital markets, and robust regulatory frameworks,
experience sustained economic growth.
• Example: (1 mark)
o India’s post-1991 liberalization led to expansion of financial
markets, diversification of instruments, and entry of private and
foreign institutions, all contributing to faster GDP growth.
Definition (2 Marks)
Securitization is the process of transforming illiquid financial assets such as
loans, receivables, or mortgages into marketable securities that can be sold to
investors.
Securitization “refers to a structured process through which financial assets
are pooled, converted into securities, and sold to investors, thereby providing
liquidity to the originator.”
Major Steps in the Securitization Process (8 Marks)
1. Origination
o The process begins when a financial institution (the originator),
such as a bank or housing finance company, creates a portfolio
of assets — for example, housing loans or auto loans.
o These assets must be homogeneous in nature and generate
predictable cash flows.
2. Pooling of Assets
o The selected assets with similar characteristics are pooled
CO-1, 2;   together to form a diversified asset base, reducing risk through
b   [10]
BL-1   aggregation.
3. Transfer to Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV)
o The originator transfers the asset pool to an independent entity
known as the Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) or Special Purpose
Entity (SPE).
o The SPV is legally separate from the originator and issues asset-
backed securities (ABS) or mortgage-backed securities (MBS)
to investors.
4. Issuance of Securities
o The SPV issues securities backed by the asset pool.
o These securities are rated by credit rating agencies and sold to
institutional and retail investors.
5. Servicing and Payment to Investors
o The servicer (often the originator) collects payments from
borrowers and transfers the proceeds to the SPV.
o The SPV then distributes the cash flows (interest and principal)
to the investors.
1. Introduction (2 marks)
Q7   The Reserve Bank of India (RBI), established in 1935 under the RBI Act,
CO-1,2;   a   1934, serves as the apex monetary authority of India. It performs regulatory [10]
BL-1, 2   and supervisory functions to ensure the stability, efficiency, and soundness of
the Indian financial system. Its main objective is to maintain monetary
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
stability, promote financial inclusion, and ensure a safe and resilient banking
and financial sector.
2. Regulatory Functions of RBI (4 marks)
The RBI formulates regulations under various Acts such as the Banking
Regulation Act, 1949 and the RBI Act, 1934. Major regulatory functions
include:
1. Monetary Policy Regulation
o RBI controls money supply and credit through instruments like
the Repo Rate, Reverse Repo Rate, CRR, and SLR.
o Example: During inflationary periods, RBI raises the repo rate
to curb excess liquidity.
2. Regulation of Banks and Financial Institutions
o   It issues licences, prescribes capital adequacy norms (Basel III),
and ensures risk management.
o Example: Implementation of Prompt Corrective Action (PCA)
framework for weak banks.
3. Regulation of Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs)
o RBI monitors NBFCs to prevent systemic risks through
registration, capital requirements, and asset classification
norms.
o Example: Post-IL&FS crisis, RBI tightened liquidity and
governance norms for NBFCs.
4. Foreign Exchange Management
o Under FEMA, 1999, RBI regulates foreign exchange markets,
ensuring stability of the Indian rupee.
o Example: Intervention in forex markets to reduce rupee
volatility.
3. Supervisory Functions of RBI (4 marks)
RBI’s supervision ensures that financial institutions operate in a sound,
ethical, and efficient manner.
1. Off-site and On-site Surveillance
o Through the Department of Supervision, RBI conducts off-site
monitoring (returns and reports) and on-site inspections of
banks.
2. Risk-Based Supervision
o Focuses on assessing institutional risk profiles rather than mere
compliance.
o Example: Introduction of the Supervisory Program for
Assessment of Risk and Capital framework.
3. Consumer Protection and Ombudsman Schemes
o RBI ensures transparency, fair practices, and redressal through
the Integrated Ombudsman Scheme.
4. Financial Stability and Development Council
o RBI plays a key role in coordination with other regulators to
maintain systemic stability.
1. Meaning of Hire-Purchase Agreement (4 marks)
A hire-purchase agreement is a form of installment credit under which goods
CO-1, 2;   are let on hire, and the hirer (buyer) agrees to pay the purchase price in regular
b   installments over a specified period.
BL-1,2
Ownership of the goods does not pass immediately to the hirer at the time of
the agreement. Instead, the hirer obtains possession and use of the goods upon
payment of the first installment. The ownership (title) is transferred only after
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
the payment of the final installment and upon the exercise of the option to
purchase.
Thus, it is a hybrid contract, combining elements of bailment and sale:
•   Initially, it is a contract of hire (bailment).
• On completion of payments, it becomes a contract of sale.
2. Transfer of Ownership (4 marks)
• Possession of goods is transferred to the hirer at the beginning of the
agreement.
• Ownership (title) remains with the owner/vendor until all installments
are paid.
• The hirer has the option to purchase the goods at the end of the hire
period by paying the final installment or a nominal option fee.
•   If the hirer defaults in payment, the owner has the right to repossess
the goods and terminate the agreement.
• On full payment and exercise of the purchase option, the title passes to
the hirer, converting the transaction into a sale.
3. Example (2 marks)
Suppose Ravi enters into a hire-purchase agreement with Bajaj Auto Finance
to acquire a motorcycle worth ₹1, 20,000.
• He pays ₹20,000 as a down payment and agrees to pay the balance ₹1,
00,000 in 20 monthly installments of ₹5,000 each.
• He gets immediate possession of the bike, but ownership remains with
the finance company.
•   After making all payments, ownership is transferred to Ravi.
•   If Ravi defaults midway, Bajaj Auto Finance can repossess the
motorcycle.
********** 12 **********
````

````text
Q1
[20]
1. Definition of Commercial Banks – 1 Mark   [05]
a.
Commercial banks are institutions that accept deposits from the public
and provide loans and advances to earn profit while ensuring liquidity
and safety. They operate under the regulation of the Reserve Bank of
India.
2. Core Functions (Deposits, Lending, Payments) – 2 Marks
• Acceptance of Deposits: Includes savings, current, and fixed
deposits.
• Lending and Credit Creation: Provide loans (cash credit,
overdraft, term loans) and create credit in the economy.
CO-1,2;
• Payment Functions: Facilitate transactions via cheques,
BL-2
NEFT, RTGS, UPI, etc., ensuring smooth money circulation.
3. Role in Economic Development & Financial Inclusion – 1 Mark
Commercial banks contribute to economic development by financing
priority sectors like agriculture and MSMEs. They also promote
financial inclusion through schemes such as Jan Dhan Yojana,
expanding banking access to rural and underprivileged populations.
4. Relevance in Financial System – 1 Mark
Commercial banks are essential for efficient allocation of financial
resources, maintaining liquidity, and ensuring financial stability,
making them indispensable to the Indian financial system.
Forfaiting (1 mark)   [05]
CO-1,2 ;   Forfaiting is a method of export financing whereby an exporter sells its
b.
BL-1   medium-   to long-term receivables   (arising from export of
goods/services) to a forfaiter (usually a financial institution) on a
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
without recourse basis, in exchange for immediate cash. The forfaiter
assumes all risks of default, interest rate, and country risk.
Mechanism of Forfaiting (2 marks)
The process typically involves the following steps:
1. Export Transaction: Exporter supplies goods to an overseas
importer on deferred payment terms.
2. Creation   of Debt Instruments: The importer   issues
promissory notes/bills of exchange, often guaranteed by a bank.
3. Sale to Forfaiter: The exporter sells these receivables to a
forfaiter at a discount.
4. Immediate Payment: The forfaiter pays the exporter upfront
(after deducting discount, fees).
5. Collection by Forfaiter: The forfaiter collects payments from
the importer on due dates and bears all risks.
3. Role in Export Finance (2 marks)
Forfaiting plays a significant role in facilitating international trade:
• Risk Mitigation: Eliminates credit, political, and exchange
risks for exporters.
• Improved Liquidity: Converts credit sales into immediate
cash, enhancing working capital.
• Competitive Advantage: Enables exporters to offer attractive
credit terms to foreign buyers.
• Balance Sheet Relief: Removes receivables, improving
financial ratios.
• Support for Capital Goods Exports: Particularly useful in
financing large-value exports with longer credit periods.
CO-1; BL-   Meaning – 1 Mark   [05]
1   c.
The International Financial System refers to the framework of
institutions, markets, instruments, rules, and regulations that facilitate
financial transactions across national boundaries. It governs the flow
of capital, foreign exchange, and financial services between countries,
enabling international trade, investment, and economic cooperation.
Features of the International Financial System: (4 Marks; Any
Four; 1 Mark Each)
1. Global Financial Institutions
The system is supported by institutions such as the International
Monetary Fund (IMF), World Bank, and Bank for International
Settlements (BIS), which ensure stability, provide financial
assistance, and promote global economic development.
2. Foreign Exchange Market
A key feature is the existence of a vast and highly liquid foreign
exchange market where currencies are traded and exchange
rates are determined.
3. International Capital Flows
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
It facilitates cross-border movement of capital in the form of
Foreign Direct Investment (FDI), Foreign Portfolio Investment
(FPI), external commercial borrowings, etc.
4. Regulatory Framework and Coordination
The system operates under international agreements, standards,
and regulations (such as Basel norms) to ensure financial
stability and reduce systemic risk.
5. Integration of Financial Markets
National financial markets are increasingly integrated, allowing
investors to diversify globally and enabling efficient allocation
of resources.
5 Marks   [05]
d.
````
