1. Definition of Commercial Banks – 1 Mark   [05]
a.
Commercial banks are institutions that accept deposits from the public
and provide loans and advances to earn profit while ensuring liquidity
and safety. They operate under the regulation of the Reserve Bank of
India.
2. Core Functions (Deposits, Lending, Payments) – 2 Marks
• Acceptance of Deposits: Includes savings, current, and fixed
deposits.
• Lending and Credit Creation: Provide loans (cash credit,
overdraft, term loans) and create credit in the economy.
CO-1,2;
• Payment Functions: Facilitate transactions via cheques,
BL-2
NEFT, RTGS, UPI, etc., ensuring smooth money circulation.
3. Role in Economic Development & Financial Inclusion – 1 Mark
Commercial banks contribute to economic development by financing
priority sectors like agriculture and MSMEs. They also promote
financial inclusion through schemes such as Jan Dhan Yojana,
expanding banking access to rural and underprivileged populations.
4. Relevance in Financial System – 1 Mark
Commercial banks are essential for efficient allocation of financial
resources, maintaining liquidity, and ensuring financial stability,
making them indispensable to the Indian financial system.
Forfaiting (1 mark)   [05]
CO-1,2 ;   Forfaiting is a method of export financing whereby an exporter sells its
b.
BL-1   medium-   to long-term receivables   (arising from export of
goods/services) to a forfaiter (usually a financial institution) on a
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
without recourse basis, in exchange for immediate cash. The forfaiter
assumes all risks of default, interest rate, and country risk.
Mechanism of Forfaiting (2 marks)
The process typically involves the following steps:
1. Export Transaction: Exporter supplies goods to an overseas
importer on deferred payment terms.
2. Creation   of Debt Instruments: The importer   issues
promissory notes/bills of exchange, often guaranteed by a bank.
3. Sale to Forfaiter: The exporter sells these receivables to a
forfaiter at a discount.
4. Immediate Payment: The forfaiter pays the exporter upfront
(after deducting discount, fees).
5. Collection by Forfaiter: The forfaiter collects payments from
the importer on due dates and bears all risks.
3. Role in Export Finance (2 marks)
Forfaiting plays a significant role in facilitating international trade:
• Risk Mitigation: Eliminates credit, political, and exchange
risks for exporters.
• Improved Liquidity: Converts credit sales into immediate
cash, enhancing working capital.
• Competitive Advantage: Enables exporters to offer attractive
credit terms to foreign buyers.
• Balance Sheet Relief: Removes receivables, improving
financial ratios.
• Support for Capital Goods Exports: Particularly useful in
financing large-value exports with longer credit periods.
CO-1; BL-   Meaning – 1 Mark   [05]
1   c.
The International Financial System refers to the framework of
institutions, markets, instruments, rules, and regulations that facilitate
financial transactions across national boundaries. It governs the flow
of capital, foreign exchange, and financial services between countries,
enabling international trade, investment, and economic cooperation.
Features of the International Financial System: (4 Marks; Any
Four; 1 Mark Each)
1. Global Financial Institutions
The system is supported by institutions such as the International
Monetary Fund (IMF), World Bank, and Bank for International
Settlements (BIS), which ensure stability, provide financial
assistance, and promote global economic development.
2. Foreign Exchange Market
A key feature is the existence of a vast and highly liquid foreign
exchange market where currencies are traded and exchange
rates are determined.
3. International Capital Flows
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
It facilitates cross-border movement of capital in the form of
Foreign Direct Investment (FDI), Foreign Portfolio Investment
(FPI), external commercial borrowings, etc.
4. Regulatory Framework and Coordination
The system operates under international agreements, standards,
and regulations (such as Basel norms) to ensure financial
stability and reduce systemic risk.
5. Integration of Financial Markets
National financial markets are increasingly integrated, allowing
investors to diversify globally and enabling efficient allocation
of resources.

a   Derivatives (2 mark)
A derivative is a financial instrument whose value is derived from the
value of an underlying asset, such as shares, bonds, commodities,
currencies,   interest   rates,   or   market   indices.
They are typically used for hedging, speculation, and arbitrage.
Features of Derivatives (3 marks)
1. Derived Value
The value depends on the underlying asset (e.g., stock, index
Q2   like Nifty 50).
CO-1,2;   2. Leverage   [10]
BL-2   Small initial margin allows taking large positions, amplifying
gains/losses.
3. Standardization (Exchange-traded)
Many derivatives (like futures) are standardized in terms of
contract size and expiry.
4. Risk Management Tool
Used to hedge against price fluctuations (e.g., currency risk,
interest rate risk).
5. Settlement Mechanism
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Can be cash-settled or settled by physical delivery.
6. Forward Commitment
Contracts involve obligations or rights to transact at a future
date.
Major Types of Financial Derivatives in India (5 marks)
1. Forwards
o Customized contracts traded OTC (Over-the-Counter).
o Agreement to buy/sell an asset at a future date at a
predetermined price.
o Example:   Currency   forwards   used   by
exporters/importers.
2. Futures
o Standardized contracts traded on exchanges like NSE
and BSE.
o Daily marking-to-market reduces default risk.
o Examples: Index futures (Nifty, Bank Nifty), stock
futures.
3. Options
o Provide the right but not obligation to buy/sell.
o Two types:
▪   Call option (right to buy)
▪ Put option (right to sell)
o Widely traded in India, especially index options.
4. Swaps
o Agreements to exchange cash flows.
o Common types:
▪   Interest rate swaps
▪ Currency swaps
o Mostly OTC instruments used by institutions.
b   Definition (2 marks)
Leasing is a financial arrangement in which the owner of an asset
(lessor) grants the right to use the asset to another party (lessee) for a
specified period in return for periodic lease rentals. It is an important
non-banking financial service in the Indian financial system,
facilitating capital formation without immediate ownership.
2. Types of Leasing (3 marks)
(i) Financial Lease
CO-1,2;
• Long-term, non-cancellable lease   [10]
BL-3
• Lessee bears risks and rewards of ownership
• Lease period covers major economic life of asset
(ii) Operating Lease
•   Short-term, cancellable lease
• Lessor bears maintenance and obsolescence risk
• Asset can be leased to multiple users
(iii) Sale and Leaseback
• Owner sells asset to lessor and leases it back
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
• Used to unlock liquidity
(iv) Leveraged Lease
• Lessor finances asset partly through debt
• Common in high-value assets (e.g., aircraft)
3. Advantages (3 marks)
For Lessee:
• No large initial investment (preserves liquidity)
• Tax benefits (lease rentals treated as expenses)
•   Flexibility in asset usage
•   Protection against obsolescence (especially operating lease)
For Lessor:
•   Stable income through lease rentals
• Tax advantages (depreciation benefits)
• Ownership of asset provides security
4. Disadvantages (2 marks)
For Lessee:
• Higher long-term cost compared to purchase
• No ownership benefits
• Fixed payment obligation even during low business activity
For Lessor:
• Risk of default by lessee
• Asset obsolescence risk (especially in operating lease)
• High initial capital requirement
Introduction (2 mark)
a   The capital market is a segment of the financial system that facilitates
the mobilization and allocation of long-term funds through instruments
such as equity shares, debentures, and bonds. It plays a pivotal role in
linking savings with productive investments, thereby fostering
economic growth.
Contribution of the Capital Market to Economic Growth (8
marks)
1. Mobilization of Savings
•   Capital markets attract savings from households and
Q3   institutions.
CO-1, 2;   • Converts idle savings into productive investments.   [10]
BL-3   • Encourages financial inclusion and investment culture.
2. Efficient Allocation of Resources
• Funds are allocated to profitable and productive sectors
through market mechanisms.
•   Price discovery ensures capital flows to efficient firms.
• Promotes optimal utilization of scarce resources.
3. Capital Formation
•   Facilitates raising of long-term funds for industrial and
infrastructural development.
• Supports expansion of industries and new ventures.
• Leads to increase in national income and output.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
4. Promotion of Industrial Growth
• Provides companies access to equity and debt financing.
• Encourages entrepreneurship and innovation.
• Supports modernization and technological advancement.
5. Liquidity and Marketability
• Secondary markets provide liquidity to investors.
• Easy entry and exit enhances investor confidence.
•   Liquidity encourages more participation, boosting investment.
6. Development of Financial Institutions
• Growth of stock exchanges, mutual funds, and intermediaries.
• Strengthens the overall financial system.
• Improves financial intermediation efficiency.
7. Encouragement of Foreign Investment Well-developed capital
markets attract Foreign Institutional Investors (FIIs) and Foreign
Direct Investment (FDI).
• Enhances capital inflows and foreign exchange reserves.
8. Wealth Creation and Economic Stability
• Provides opportunities for wealth generation through
investments.
•   Facilitates risk diversification.
•   Contributes to economic stability and growth sustainability.
Credit Rating (3 marks)
b
Credit Rating refers to an independent and objective assessment of the
creditworthiness of a borrower or a financial instrument, indicating the
ability and willingness to repay debt obligations (principal and interest)
on time.
It is expressed through symbolic grades (such as AAA, AA, A, BBB,
etc.) assigned by credit rating agencies like CRISIL, ICRA, CARE, and
India Ratings.
• AAA indicates highest safety (lowest risk)
• Lower ratings indicate higher risk of default
Thus, credit rating acts as a risk indicator for investors.
Importance of Credit Rating in the Indian Financial System (7
CO-1,2 ;
marks)   [10]
BL-2
1. Facilitates Investment Decisions
Credit ratings provide a simple and reliable basis for investors to
evaluate risk, especially for retail investors who may lack expertise.
2. Enhances Market Efficiency
By reducing information asymmetry, ratings ensure better price
discovery and efficient allocation of financial resources.
3. Promotes Corporate Discipline
Borrowers strive to maintain good ratings, encouraging financial
discipline, transparency, and sound management practices.
4. Aids Regulatory Framework
Regulators like SEBI and RBI often use ratings for investment
guidelines and prudential norms, especially for institutional investors.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
5. Reduces Cost of Borrowing
Firms with higher ratings can raise funds at lower interest rates, thereby
reducing their cost of capital.
6. Supports the Development of the Debt Market
Credit rating is essential for the growth of corporate bond markets in
India, as it builds investor confidence.
7. Protects Investor Interests
It acts as a safeguard by warning investors against risky securities,
thereby minimizing chances of default losses.
Microfinance (3 Marks)
a
Microfinance refers to the provision of a wide range of financial
services, such as small loans (microcredit), savings, insurance, and
remittance facilities—to low-income individuals or economically
weaker sections who lack access to formal banking channels.
Microfinance is a tool for financial inclusion, aimed at enabling the
poor to become self-reliant by supporting income-generating activities
and reducing poverty.
(a) Formation of SHGs (2 Marks)
• An SHG is a voluntary association of 10–20 individuals,
typically from similar socio-economic backgrounds.
• Members pool small savings regularly into a common fund.
• The group is often promoted by NGOs, banks, or government
agencies (e.g., NABARD initiative).
•   After demonstrating financial discipline, the group becomes
eligible for bank linkage and credit.
Q4   (b) Salient Features (3 Marks)
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-2   • Group-based approach: Peer pressure ensures repayment
discipline.
• Savings-led model: Credit is preceded by regular savings habits.
•   Collateral-free loans: Lending is based on mutual trust rather
than physical collateral.
•   Financial inclusion: Connects the unbanked population to
formal banking institutions.
• Democratic functioning: Decisions are taken collectively by
members.
(c) Objectives (2 Marks)
• To promote financial inclusion among the rural and urban poor.
• To encourage savings and credit discipline.
• To support self-employment and income generation.
• To empower women socially and economically (as many SHGs
are women-centric).
• To reduce dependence on moneylenders and informal credit
sources.
The Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India
CO-1, 2;
b   (IRDAI) is the apex statutory body established under the IRDA Act, [10]
BL-4
1999, to regulate, promote, and ensure the orderly growth of the
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
insurance sector in India. It plays a crucial role in safeguarding
policyholders’ interests while fostering a competitive and efficient
insurance market.
Role of IRDAI (4 Marks)
• Acts as the regulator and supervisor of the insurance industry in
India.
• Ensures financial stability, transparency, and fairness in
insurance operations.
•   Protects the interests of policyholders and builds confidence in
the insurance system.
• Promotes the development and penetration of insurance in both
urban and rural areas.
Functions of IRDAI (6 Marks)
1. Regulatory Functions
•   Issues licenses/registration to insurance companies and
intermediaries.
• Frames rules, regulations, and guidelines for the conduct of
insurance business.
• Monitors compliance with legal and financial norms.
2. Protection of Policyholders
• Ensures fair treatment of policyholders.
• Regulates terms and conditions of insurance policies.
• Provides mechanisms for grievance redressal.
3. Financial Supervision
• Maintains solvency margins to ensure insurers can meet
obligations.
• Conducts inspections, audits, and investigations of insurers.
• Ensures prudent investment of insurance funds.
4. Promotion and Development
• Encourages growth and innovation in insurance products.
• Promotes insurance penetration in rural and social sectors.
•   Facilitates healthy competition among insurers.
5. Regulation of Intermediaries
• Supervises   agents,   brokers,   surveyors, and   third-party
administrators.
•   Sets qualification, training, and code of conduct standards.
6. Consumer Education and Awareness
• Spreads awareness about insurance products and rights of
policyholders.
a   Role of Development Financial Institutions in Agriculture and
Rural Development (10 Marks; any five; 2 Marks each)
Q5   1. Provision of Long-term and Medium-term Finance
CO-1,2;   [10]
BL-2   DFIs such as NABARD provide refinance facilities to banks for
agricultural investment (e.g., irrigation, farm mechanization, land
development).
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
• Unlike commercial banks, DFIs focus on developmental
lending, not merely profitability.
• They support capital formation in agriculture.
2. Institutional Credit Support and Rural Credit Structure
DFIs strengthen the rural credit delivery system by:
• Supporting cooperative banks, Regional Rural Banks (RRBs),
and commercial banks
• Ensuring adequate and timely credit flow to farmers and rural
enterprises
This   reduces dependence on   informal   sources   like
moneylenders.
3. Promotion of Rural Infrastructure Development
Through instruments like the Rural Infrastructure Development Fund
(RIDF):
• Financing roads, irrigation, bridges, warehousing
• Enhancing agricultural productivity and rural connectivity
4. Developmental and Promotional Functions
DFIs go beyond finance by:
• Supporting Self-Help Groups (SHGs) and microfinance
• Encouraging financial inclusion
• Promoting rural entrepreneurship and skill development
5. Policy Planning and Coordination Role
DFIs act as apex institutions:
•   Assist in policy formulation for agriculture and rural sectors
• Coordinate between government, RBI, and financial institutions
• Provide research, consultancy, and training support
6. Risk Mitigation and Credit Innovation
• Promote crop insurance, credit guarantee schemes
• Encourage innovative products like Kisan Credit Cards (KCC)
• Help reduce risks inherent in agriculture
Credit Card (2 Marks)
b
A credit card is a payment instrument issued by banks or financial
institutions that allows the cardholder to purchase goods and services
on credit, up to a pre-approved limit. The user is required to repay the
borrowed amount either fully within a stipulated period (grace period)
or partially with interest.
It is essentially a revolving line of credit, enabling repeated borrowing
CO-1, 2;   within the sanctioned limit.
[10]
BL-2   Features of Credit Card (4 Marks; any four; 1 Mark Each)
1. Credit Limit
Each cardholder is assigned a maximum borrowing limit based
on income, creditworthiness, and repayment capacity.
2. Grace Period
A specified period (usually 20–50 days) during which no
interest is charged if the full amount is repaid.
3. Minimum Payment Facility
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
Cardholders can pay a minimum amount due, carrying forward
the balance with interest.
4. Interest Charges
High interest rates are charged on outstanding balances if full
payment is not made.
5. Billing Cycle
Transactions are recorded in a monthly statement showing
purchases, dues, and payment deadlines.
6. Security Features
Includes PIN, CVV, OTP authentication, and EMV chip for
secure transactions.
Uses of Credit Card (4 Marks; any four; 1 Mark Each)
1. Purchasing Goods and Services
Enables cashless transactions both offline and online.
2. Short-term Credit Facility
Helps manage liquidity by deferring payments.
3. Emergency Funding
Useful in urgent financial needs when cash is not readily
available.
4. Cash Withdrawal (Cash Advance)
Allows withdrawal of cash from ATMs, though with higher
charges.
5. Rewards and Benefits
Offers cashback, reward points, travel benefits, and discounts.
a   (a) Formal Financial System (2.5 Marks)
The formal financial system refers to the organized, regulated, and
institutionally structured financial framework in India. It operates
under the supervision of regulatory authorities such as the Reserve
Bank of India (RBI), SEBI, and others.
It includes:
• Commercial banks (public and private sector)
• Cooperative banks
• Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs)
•   Financial markets (money and capital markets)
• Insurance companies and mutual funds
Key features:
Q6
CO-1   • Governed by laws and regulations   [10]
BL-3   • Transparent operations
• Formal documentation and procedures
•   Institutional credit delivery
(b) Informal Financial System (2.5 Marks)
The informal financial system comprises unorganized and
unregulated financial activities that operate outside the purview of
formal regulatory institutions.
It includes:
• Moneylenders
• Indigenous bankers
•   Chit funds and rotating savings groups
•   Friends, relatives, and local lenders
Key features:
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
• No formal regulation
•   Flexible and quick transactions
• Based on trust or personal relationships
• Often high interest rates
Reasons for Continued Existence of Informal Financial System (5
Marks)
Despite the expansion of the formal financial sector, the informal
system persists due to the following reasons:
(1) Accessibility and Convenience
Formal institutions often involve lengthy procedures, documentation,
and eligibility criteria. Informal sources provide quick and hassle-
free credit, especially in emergencies.
(2) Lack of Financial Inclusion
A significant portion of India’s rural and semi-urban population still
lacks access to formal banking services. This gap sustains dependence
on informal sources.
(3) Flexibility in Lending
Informal lenders offer:
•   Flexible repayment schedules
• No collateral requirements
• Customized loan terms
This is particularly beneficial for small borrowers and farmers.
(4) Socio-cultural Factors
Borrowers often rely on trust-based relationships, familiarity, and
traditional practices, which formal institutions cannot replicate.
(5) Inadequate Credit Delivery by Formal Sector
Formal institutions sometimes fail to meet:
• Timely credit needs
•   Small-ticket loans
• Risky borrower segments
Debt Instruments (1.5 marks)
b
Debt instruments are financial claims through which borrowers
(government, corporations, and financial institutions) raise funds from
lenders with a promise to repay principal along with interest. In the
Indian financial system, they include government securities (G-Secs),
treasury bills, corporate bonds, debentures, commercial paper (CP),
and certificates of deposit (CDs).
They form the backbone of the fixed income market, complementing
equity markets and ensuring stability in capital formation.
CO-1, 2;
[10]
BL-2   Features of Debt Instruments (2.5 marks)
• Fixed Income   Nature: Provide   predetermined   returns
(coupon/interest).
• Maturity Period: Ranges from short-term (T-bills, CP) to long-
term (bonds, debentures).
•   Credit Risk Variation: Depends on issuer (sovereign vs
corporate).
•   Tradability & Liquidity: Many instruments are tradable in
secondary markets (e.g., G-Sec market).
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
•   Priority in Claims: Debt holders have priority over equity
holders during liquidation.
•   Interest Rate Sensitivity: Prices inversely related to interest rate
movements.
Uses of Debt Instruments (2 marks)
• Government Financing: Fiscal deficit funding through G-Secs
and T-bills.
• Corporate Funding: Companies raise long-term capital via
bonds/debentures.
• Liquidity Management: Short-term instruments (CP, CDs)
help manage working capital.
• Monetary Policy Transmission: RBI uses debt market
operations (OMO, repo) to influence liquidity.
• Investment Avenue: Provides stable returns for institutional
and retail investors (e.g., pension funds, banks).
Significance in Financial Market Development (2 marks)
• Deepens Financial Markets: A well-developed bond market
• Benchmark Yield Curve Formation: G-Sec yields act as
reference rates for pricing other instruments.
•   Efficient Capital Allocation: Channels savings into productive
investments.
• Risk Diversification: Offers investors alternatives to equity.
• Infrastructure Financing: Long-term bonds support large-
scale projects.
Limitations & Challenges (2 marks)
• Underdeveloped Corporate Bond Market: Compared to
advanced economies, India’s corporate debt market lacks depth.
• Liquidity   Constraints: Secondary market   trading   is
concentrated in G-Secs.
• Credit Rating Dependence: Over-reliance on rating agencies
may mask risks.
•   Retail Participation is Low: Limited awareness and access
barriers.
•   Interest Rate Volatility: Affects bond valuation and investor
confidence.
a   The Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI), established in
1988 and given statutory powers under the SEBI Act, 1992, is the
principal regulator of the Indian capital market. It ensures investor
protection, promotes market development, and regulates securities
Q7   markets to ensure fairness and transparency.
CO-1,2;   1. Role of SEBI (3 Marks)   [10]
BL-3   SEBI plays a threefold role in the Indian financial system:
1. Protector of Investors
o Safeguards investor interests by ensuring disclosure,
transparency, and preventing malpractices like insider
trading and fraud.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
2. Regulator of Securities Market
o Regulates stock exchanges, intermediaries (brokers,
merchant bankers), and listed companies to maintain
orderly functioning.
3. Promoter of Market Development
o Encourages innovation (e.g., derivatives, mutual funds),
improves market infrastructure, and enhances financial
inclusion.
Functions of SEBI (4 Marks)
SEBI’s functions can be classified into protective, regulatory, and
developmental functions:
(A) Protective Functions
•   Prohibits insider trading and unfair trade practices
• Promotes investor education and awareness
• Ensures fair treatment of investors
(B) Regulatory Functions
•   Registers and regulates intermediaries (brokers, mutual funds,
credit rating agencies)
• Regulates stock exchanges and trading practices
• Approves public issues and monitors compliance with
disclosure norms
• Enforces corporate governance standards
(C) Developmental Functions
• Promotes self-regulatory organizations (SROs)
•   Introduces technological reforms   (e.g., online   trading,
dematerialization)
• Expands market instruments and deepens capital markets
Powers of SEBI (3 Marks)
SEBI has wide statutory powers under the SEBI Act, 1992:
1. Quasi-Legislative Powers
o Frames rules and regulations for market participants
2. Quasi-Judicial Powers
o Conducts inquiries and adjudicates disputes
o Can impose penalties, suspend or cancel registrations
3. Quasi-Executive Powers
o Conducts inspections, audits, and investigations
o Can issue directions to protect investor interests
o Has authority to search, seize, and enforce compliance
Hire purchase is a method of asset acquisition where the buyer pays an
b
initial down payment and the balance in instalments. Ownership is
transferred only after the final payment, while possession is available
CO-1, 2;
immediately. (2Marks)   [10]
BL-3
Example
A small transport operator purchases a truck worth ₹10 lakh under a
hire purchase agreement. He pays ₹2 lakh upfront and the remaining
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---
₹8 lakh in monthly instalments over 4 years. He can use the truck to
generate income even before becoming its legal owner.
Advantages of Hire Purchase (Any four; 4 Marks; 1 Mark Each)
1. Facilitates Asset Acquisition - Enables individuals or firms
with limited funds to acquire expensive assets without full
upfront payment.
2. Immediate Possession and Use -The hirer can use the asset to
generate income while paying instalments.
3. Flexible Payment Structure- Instalments can be structured
according to the borrower’s cash flow.
4. No Additional Collateral required- The asset itself serves as
security, reducing the need for external collateral.
5. Tax Benefits -Depreciation and interest components may be
tax-deductible for businesses.
Disadvantages of Hire Purchase (Any four; 4 Marks; 1 Mark Each)
1. Higher Overall Cost -Total cost is higher than outright
purchase due to interest charges.
2. Ownership Risk -Ownership remains with the seller until full
payment; default may lead to repossession.
3. Rigid Payment Obligations -Failure to meet instalments can
result in penalties or loss of asset.
4. Limited Freedom of Disposal -The hirer cannot sell or transfer
the asset before full payment.
5. Complex Documentation -Legal formalities and terms may be
complicated for small borrowers.
